Trump raises prospect of 'big, beautiful' Russia-Ukraine deal

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

US President Donald Trump said the Ukraine war was close to being settled and that ending it might involve "all of Europe." Trump has previously made several similar promises, with no resolution to the conflict in sight.

US President Donald Trump on Thursday said a "very big" deal is in the works with Russia to end the Ukraine war.

Trump made the comments after he had an unscheduled meeting with Russian ⁠President Vladimir Putin's special envoy Kirill Dmitriev at the White House.

Speaking to ​reporters later, the president said the Russia-Ukraine war was ​very close to being settled and that bringing it to an end might involve "all of Europe."

"I can't tell you what I discussed. But I'm just telling you, it's going to be an agreement, it's going to be a big agreement," Trump said in the Oval Office.

"It's going to be very big. It's going to involve more than Ukraine, and I could even say it might involve all of Europe. It's going to be a big, beautiful agreement, and everyone's going to be thrilled."

Trump said he had not met with Ukrainian officials who were also at the White House on Thursday.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

Meanwhile, Trump's son-in-law and US envoy Jared Kushner said the United States, NATO, ⁠and European ⁠partners held ​"productive and substantive ​discussions" on resolving ​the war in Ukraine Thursday.

"Our objective is to address the security concerns of all sides through practical, mutually agreed arrangements that reduce the risk of future conflict," he wrote on X, ​ahead of ⁠discussions ​next ​week in ​Abu Dhabi involving Kyiv and ​Moscow.

Even before his return to the White House, Trump has made similar promises regarding an imminent end to the conflict, famously saying he would end the war in one day upon becoming president.

Delegations from both Russia and Ukraine were at the White House on Thursday for an event honoring First Lady Melania Trump's work brokering a recent ceasefire in the war-ravaged city of Oleshky.

Speaking at her invitation, Russian envoy Dmitriev thanked the first lady for her "distinguished humanitarian role" in securing the six-day truce.

Dmitriev added that Putin personally gave instructions as part of the operation and sent his thanks to Melania Trump.

The head of the parliamentary faction of President Volodymyr Zelenskyy's party, Davyd Arakhamia, and Ukraine's human rights ombudsman, Dmytro Lubinets, also spoke at the event.

Arakhamia told the first lady she did an "amazing job" and encouraged her to keep helping the Ukrainian people.

"Definitely we would like to ask you to keep going and to find new missions and to de-escalate more as much as possible," Arakhamia said.

Asian stocks slump after US tech rout amid AI jitters

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Concerns that Democrats in the US will push back against data center projects are weighing on an AI trade that is already on shaky footing amid growing doubts about profitability.

Japanese and South Korean shares tumbled on Friday, following a massive selloff in tech stocks on Wall Street the previous day.

Tokyo's benchmark Nikkei 225 index fell 5.5% in early trade, while Seoul's tech-heavy Kospi index slid as much as 8.8%, triggering a circuit breaker which suspended trading for 20 minutes. Taiwan's benchmark TAIEX index was also down more than 8%.

Asian tech giants including Samsung Electronics, SK Hynix and Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing Company (TSMC) have borne the brunt of the artificial intelligence-related selloff.

Samsung plunged nearly 10% while SK Hynix slid over 11%.

Chip-related shares in Japan also took a beating, with Tokyo Electron stock trading down around 9% and Kioxia shedding 13%.

European stock futures fell 1%, indicating that the selloff was likely to spread across to Europe.

To view this video please enable JavaScript, and consider upgrading to a web browser that supports HTML5 video

On Thursday, US markets were hammered by a sharp retreat in tech stocks, particularly those involved in the buildout of artificial intelligence infrastructure like data centers.

The tech-heavy Nasdaq fell 5.52% and shed as much as $2.5 trillion (roughly €2.23 trillion) in market value, while the broader S&P 500 index plunged 4.4%.

The rout occurred as investors grow increasingly anxious about the colossal AI spending plans and whether they will eventually produce commensurate profits.

The results of the US midterm elections, which saw Democrats seize control of both houses of US Congress, added to the concerns.

A number of Democratic governors-elect campaigned on slowing artificial intelligence development and promised to stop or pause the construction of data centers amid growing public opposition to such projects.

"It's pretty clear there's been a real shift ​in sentiment," Michael McCarthy, chief executive for Australia and New Zealand at online brokerage Moomoo, told the Reuters news agency.

"Market turning points are often accompanied by strong disagreement, and that shows up in the market ​by huge swings in either direction," he said. "That's what we appear to have had — and last night's emphatic sell-down suggests that is the direction that we're now headed in."

Ex-lawmaker jailed in Macau's first national security trial

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Pro-democracy former lawmaker Au Kam San was found guilty of "subversion against the political power of the State." He was handed a prison sentence of 12-and-a-half years.

A court in Macau on Friday sentenced former lawmaker and pro-democracy campaigner Au Kam San to 12 years and six months in jail in the Chinese territory's first national security case.

The 69-year-old was convicted of several offences including "subversion against the political power of the State".

Au was accused of providing false information to anti-China organizations outside Macau to incite hatred against the city's government and Beijing.

The teacher-turned-lawmaker was detained in July 2025, in what was the first publicly known prosecution under Macau's national security law since it was implemented in 2009.

Au "personally provided, over a long period of time, assistance and collaboration to external hostile organisations in the collection, preparation, and public dissemination of various images and information that were manifestly filled with political claims of a subversive nature against the political authority of the State," a court statement said.

The court added that Au also sought to "incite hatred among Macau residents against the Central People's Government, with the intent to overthrow and undermine the fundamental system of the State enshrined in the Constitution of the People's Republic of China."

Au's closed-door trial — which ended in September in three days —  had raised concerns about fairness.

An open trial was ruled out by a judge to safeguard national security interests, the court had said earlier.

Critics say that Au's case illustrates China's emphasis on security and its intolerance of dissent, even though Macau's pro-democracy movement has never been as influential as its neighbor Hong Kong.

Last year, the  European Uniondenounced Au's arrest, saying it added to the concerns about the "erosion of political pluralism" in Macau.

Au served in Macau's legislature dominated by pro-China loyalists for two decades and stepped down in 2021.

He co-organized Macau's vigils commemorating the 1989 Tiananmen Square crackdown in Beijing.

Macau is a former Portuguese colony which reverted to China in 1999. Like Hong Kong, it operates as a special administrative region of China.

Pope begins lengthy trip to Latin America weeks after lung cancer surgery

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Pope Leo is travelling to Uruguay, Argentina and Peru

Pope Leo XIV is beginning a lengthy trip to Latin America just three weeks after surgery for lung cancer - a tour that includes a much-anticipated "homecoming" to Peru where he was a priest for many years.

The Vatican says no extraordinary measures are being taken in relation to the Pope's health, describing him as a "71-year-old who has undergone an operation" not someone with a "permanent medical condition".

It was Leo himself who revealed last month that doctors had discovered a tumour during a routine scan. The growth was removed along with the lower lobe of his right lung and his medical team then confirmed the cancer had not spread.

At a briefing in Rome ahead of the trip, which takes in Uruguay and Argentina as well as Peru, a spokeswoman stressed that Pope Leo had "fantastic stamina" and was "extremely enthusiastic" about the schedule which he had "personally confirmed... with no changes".

"The Vatican's message with this trip is, 'that was a short episode and now we're back on track'," Professor Massimo Faggioli of Villanova University told the BBC, pointing out that the Pope has had a busy return to work since his surgery.

"We have seen him in these last few days making speeches, audiences and so on. He seems in great shape. That was important."

On Friday Pope Leo presided over the annual memorial Mass for cardinals and bishops who have died

The 12-day tour would be demanding for anyone, though. It incorporates multiple long-haul and local flights and covers thousands of miles from the Amazon to the Andes.

The starting point will be Uruguay, where the number of Catholics has fallen away steeply in recent years and which is getting its first Papal visit in four decades.

Leo then heads for Argentina, homeland of Pope Francis, his predecessor.

Father Fernando Laguna said his heart was filled with joy when the Pope's visit to Argentina was announced

"This visit is also a homage to Francis, who didn't make it to Argentina [as pope]," says Father Fernando Laguna, the rector of Santa Maria Addolorata, Argentina's national church in Rome.

"When this visit by Pope Leo was announced, it filled all of our hearts with joy," he told the BBC. "I always thought he would go, despite his illness. I don't think he ever thought of calling it off!"

The priest is installing a big screen inside the church in Rome to allow parishioners to follow all the key moments of the trip from afar. The building is already decked out with posters declaring Pope Leo is welcome in Argentina. "We're waiting for you!", one proclaims.

'Welcome Pope Leo' reads this poster in the Argentinian church in Rome

This leg of the Pope's journey - which includes meetings with heads of state as well as religious events - could be the most politically sensitive. President Javier Milei once called Pope Francis "evil on earth" and a "communist" for promoting a doctrine of social justice to help the poor. In turn, Argentina's bishops have criticised Milei's harsh austerity measures, making sweeping cuts to social spending.

But Milei has already welcomed Pope Leo, writing in a post on X that Argentina awaits "with immense joy". Millions are expected to turn out to greet him.

The highlight of the trip is undoubtedly saved for last, when Pope Leo reaches Peru - described by the Vatican as a return to his roots.

"He knows these streets," is how a spokeswoman put it.

Worshippers at Chiclayo cathedral in Peru were delighted when their former bishop became Pope in 2025

Born in the US, the then Robert Prevost spent almost 20 years in Peru, on and off, and even has Peruvian citizenship. That time is seen as fundamental to shaping his papacy.

His schedule includes a return to the diocese of Chiclayo where he was ordained bishop in 2014 and remained until Francis moved him to the Vatican almost a decade later.

He will also travel to Cusco, former capital of the Inca Empire, to say Mass more than 3,000m above sea level before heading to Pucallpa in the Amazon.

In all three countries, the Vatican says the Pope will have meetings with survivors of clerical sexual abuse - an issue which clouded a 2018 visit to Chile and Peru by his predecessor.

"This is how the papacy reconnects with Latin America after Francis, whose visits to the region were not always seamless," is how Professor Faggioli sees it.

He believes Pope Leo has a strong sense of the unity of the Americas as one continent. "Democracies are in trouble there, so there are so many things he can address," he suggests.

"The big question then is the United States. There's no question he wants to go. So will he travel when [President Donald] Trump is still in office? I'm sure they're thinking about this."

White House denies Hegseth and Rubio to leave jobs after Republican election losses

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

The White House has denied rumours that Defence Secretary Pete Hegseth and Secretary of State Marco Rubio might leave their jobs in a post-election shakeup of the Trump administration.

US media have reported there are calls to replace Hegseth - in particular over his handling of the Iran war, which is being blamed for heavy Republican election losses.

One outgoing congressional Republican has threatened to force an impeachment vote against Hegseth as early as next week. The defence secretary posted a video statement defending his record and denouncing the effort.

"You see, they attack me. They attack us because we are effective," he said. "It's not going to work. We will not back down."

Vance walks Trump tightrope with scathing election critique blaming Iran war

Meanwhile, Rubio is denying reports he is considering leaving his post ahead of a 2028 presidential run.

Working for Trump until the end of his term has "always been my plan," Rubio told The New York Times in an interview.

"I love the work I'm doing," Rubio said. "And plus I always laugh when they say he's going to leave his job. I go, 'Which one?'"

The White House and US State Department also shut down reports that Rubio might be leaving after this week's midterm losses, with both calling it "fake news".

The defence secretary, though, faces a more uncertain political future.

Trump and Vice-President JD Vance have both blamed the Iran war for Republican losses that allowed Democrats full control of the US House of Representatives and the Senate.

In a telephone interview with ABC News, Trump said that he had received "calls about" Hegseth, but added that he had "every intention" of keeping him.

Later on Thursday, he told reporters at the White House that "Pete Hegseth has done a fantastic job", and went on to list what he sees are some of his achievements, including the arrest of Venezuelan leader NicolÃ¡s Maduro in January and the Iran war, now into its ninth month.

The Washington Post reported on Thursday that Trump might be, however, considering ousting General Dan Caine, the chairman of the Joint Chiefs of Staff, or Centcom chief Admiral Brad Cooper. Both played significant roles in offering guidance on the war, which started eight months ago.

Meanwhile, Republican Representative Thomas Massie, a frequent critic of Trump, has said he will push ahead with plans to push for Hegseth's impeachment.

He told news site Politico that he will trigger his resolution to impeach the defence secretary early next week.

And on X, he warned that the president should replace Hegseth before a new Democratic-controlled Congress forms in January.

"He should do it while he can still get the replacements confirmed in the Senate," Massie wrote on X.

Could Democrats in Congress end the war in Iran?

Hegseth has also been rumoured to be interested in running for an open Tennessee Senate seat.

In his video statement, Hegseth denounced the "sham articles of impeachment" effort against him from a "disgruntled, defeated congressman, along with all his Democrat allies", and listed ways the department has fulfilled Trump's America First agenda under his leadership.

That includes, he said, reviving military recruitment, rebuilding the defence industrial base, and rooting out diversity initiatives.

Of the Democrats, he said: "Their policies don't work. So their obsession with political theatre disguised as power drives them to try to destroy their opponents. It's all about the politics of personal destruction."

'Leaders all over the world understand that Trump is only there for two more years & then he's gone'

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

In American politics, the possession of presidential power does not necessarily guarantee the capacity to exercise it. Few contemporary leaders have embodied this contradiction as dramatically as Donald Trump. Following what political scientist Ian Bremmer describes as the most consequential electoral reversal for a governing party in more than half a century, the founder of Eurasia Group and GZERO Media argues that the United States has entered a new political phase: the beginning of the end of Trump's capacity to dominate the domestic and international agenda. In this wide-ranging interview with France 24, Bremmer revisits his provocative thesis that America has already reached "peak Trump." Far from suggesting that the president has become powerless, he contends that his authority is increasingly constrained by congressional opposition, Republican discontent, unpopular foreign policy decisions and the growing awareness among international leaders that the present administration has a limited political horizon. The historical reversal is, in Bremmer's account, extraordinary: "Trump back in 2024 engineered for himself the biggest political comeback in American political history. And now, only two years later, the biggest loss in 50 years." At the heart of his analysis lies a fundamental distinction between institutional authority and political influence. Trump remains commander-in-chief, retains the instruments of executive government and continues to command extraordinary media attention. Yet Bremmer argues that the president's ability to translate those advantages into effective political outcomes is diminishing. The consequences, he suggests, extend far beyond Washington. From the war in Iran and the unresolved conflict in Ukraine to the future of transatlantic relations, America's allies and adversaries are beginning to adjust their strategic calculations to a post-Trump political landscape. As Bremmer puts it: "Those foreign leaders will start to engage more with the America that they expect to deal with after Trump is gone." Equally significant is his diagnosis of the domestic forces driving this transformation. Economic disillusionment, unfulfilled electoral promises and accusations of political self-interest have, in his assessment, undermined the very coalition that propelled Trump back to the White House. Meanwhile, the president's apparent preoccupation with monumental projects and personal legacy raises a deeper question about the relationship between political spectacle and the responsibilities of governance. Bremmer's conclusion is unambiguous: "Yes, two more years to go. But nowhere near the level of disruption that he had on the global stage at the beginning of this term." The interview ultimately raises a question of profound significance for American democracy and the international order: Can a president retain the formal powers of the world's most consequential political office while progressively losing the political authority necessary to shape its future? Bremmer believes that process is already underway.

Trump signs order preventing future presidents from changing Oval Office

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

US President Donald Trump has signed an executive order that he says will prevent future presidents from making certain changes to the White House Oval Office.

Trump, a former real estate mogul, has drastically altered the look of the president's official office during his second term, most notably with adding many gold features.

"We're making it a national monument, and it will not be allowed to be touched by anybody, destroyed, ripped down by stupid people, in order to build something in a slightly different shape," Trump told reporters on Thursday.

The order comes months after Trump tore down the entirety of the White House East Wing in a project that has faced legal challenges.

The project was also approved by the US Congress. Construction has continued, with plans to turn the space into a new ballroom.

American presidents currently have the power to make some changes to the White House but large projects require the permission of Congress.

The executive order, which Trump signed on Thursday, declares the Oval Office to be a national monument, along with some of its surrounding rooms.

The document also says that it does not "restrict the President's authority to redecorate the monument, select furnishings, or make ordinary operational and security modifications that do not substantially impair the monument's historic structure or architectural character".

"We like the Oval Office, and it's a symbol, like the White House itself. It's a symbol of our country and the greatness of our country," Trump said, adding that world leaders who have visited in recent months have complimented his decorative changes to the room.

Trump said the White House and the Oval Office had been in need of repairs before his changes, calling it "badly damaged". He described broken windows, cracked walls and broken stone.

Many of the noticeable changes, though, were cosmetic, including a large gold plaque reading "Oval Office" outside the space, paving over the Rose Garden and adding a new helicopter pad.

Trump said the order would "make it impossible for anybody ever to destroy this incredible space".

How Trump is remaking the White House in his own image

The Oval Office in 2025 during Trump's second term in the White House

The Oval Office in 2024 during Joe Biden's tenure

The office in 2015 during Barack Obama's time in the White House

The entire building is already designated as a National Historic Landmark, which is intended to prevent against sudden changes to the building.

As president, Trump has the power to create national monuments, which are similar to national parks.

Trump's order calls on the office to be maintained by the National Park Service "in a manner that protects its historic character, architectural integrity, and association with the Presidency".

It notes the existing protections to the White House, but says "nonetheless, the Oval Office merits specific recognition and protective management as a discrete historic structure of national significance".

Congress has carved out an exemption to the National Historic Preservation Act, in order to allow presidents to make needed changes. The current Oval Office has existed since the early 1930s, after its conception by President Franklin D Roosevelt.

Greg Werkheiser, from the law firm Cultural Heritage Partners, which specialises in historic preservation, says that temporary alterations like "furniture and decor is completely up to the president's discretion".

"As long as they're not damaging the permanent art and architecture, they can generally do whatever they want," he told the BBC.

A new gold sign was placed outside the Oval Office

A Trump administration staff member seen inside the Oval Office in May

Werkheiser argues that the Trump administration has shown disregard for other historic sites in Washington and, through lawsuits, has attempted to undermine the laws that require preservation.

Trump has undertaken a massive beautification project in Washington and has held three events in the past week to boast of the construction, even as his Republican party was trounced by Democrats in the midterm elections.

Werkheiser called Trump's order "hypocrisy", noting the various projects he's pushed for across Washington - including renovating the Kennedy Center and plans to build a new triumphal arch in the city.

"On the one hand he's talking about the importance of preserving the Oval Office - which is legally meaningless because he can't do it - and on the other hand he is absolutely taking an axe to the law that allows us to preserve other historic sites."

In October, the president suggested in an interview with Time, external magazine that the Oval Office would need to be torn down due to security concerns from the US Secret Service.

"I said, 'My ass, you can't build a new one. This is a special place,'" Trump said at the time.

During the midterm election campaign, Democrats criticised Trump's redesigns, with former President Barack Obama saying that his golden tweaks have "got the White House looking like Saddam Hussein opened a Cheesecake Factory".

Nintendo To Continue Delivering Movies Beyond Live-Action Zelda, Unsurprisingly

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Nintendo is now fully committed to the movie business thanks to Super Mario and the upcoming live-action Zelda film, due out 30th April 2027.

During the latest financial briefing, company president Shuntaro Furukawa has reiterated how the Japanese firm intends to "continue delivering movies" with its partners beyond the Zelda film - while also reassuring fans that each film will be "carefully" crafted.

The plan is to also establish a "framework", allowing for ongoing production. Here's the graphic attached to these comments:

"As announced, a live-action movie of The Legend of Zelda is scheduled for release in 2027, and we plan to continue delivering movies in the years that follow.

"We will work with our partners to carefully craft each film, while establishing a framework that supports ongoing production."

The Galaxy Movie has already provided enough evidence of another Mario movie, but Nintendo hasn't officially said anything.

There have also been some rumours about the Zelda movie going beyond a single film, but the first one still needs to come out (and make bank at the global box office).

Furukawa has also issued a reminder about how the combined worldwide box office sales of the animated Mario Movies have now exceeded two billion US dollars, further expanding Nintendo's reach to new audiences and regions.

"We believe these movies have provided an opportunity for people to get to know Mario, including people in regions where our dedicated video game platform business has had limited reach and people who do not normally play games."

Manchester United are considering a surprise January move for Manchester City defender Rayan Aït-Nouri as they continue searching for a long-term solution at left-back.

The Algeria international has endured a frustrating season at the Etihad, making just four appearances in all competitions and failing to feature in the Premier League.

With regular football proving difficult to find under Enzo Maresca, the 25-year-old is now expected to push for a winter departure.

United’s interest is particularly intriguing given the rivalry between the two clubs and their need to eventually replace Luke Shaw, whose availability has repeatedly been disrupted by injuries.

According to The Sun’s report on Man United’s interest in Aït-Nouri, United are exploring whether a January deal could be possible after previously rejecting the chance to sign the defender during the summer.

City were reportedly prepared to discuss a loan containing an obligation to buy, but United were unwilling to accept those terms at the time. The situation has now changed, with Aït-Nouri increasingly open to leaving because of his lack of minutes.

United have also monitored Newcastle’s Lewis Hall, Bournemouth defender Adrien Truffert and Barcelona’s Alejandro Balde as potential long-term options on the left.

A related CaughtOffside report states that Aït-Nouri would be open to joining United and that personal terms are unlikely to present a major problem. The bigger challenge would be convincing City to strengthen their local rivals.

From United’s perspective, Aït-Nouri would be an interesting opportunity if the price is sensible.

He already has significant Premier League experience from his time with Wolves, can operate as either a traditional left-back or wing-back and, at 25, should still have his best years ahead of him.

His camp has made the frustration around his City situation clear, with a source close to the player saying his “talent is being wasted” because of his lack of opportunities.

The obvious difficulty is getting City to sanction a direct move across Manchester. Such transfers are rare and would inevitably attract attention.

However, United need more competition and durability on the left side of defence. If Aït-Nouri is genuinely available in January, ignoring the opportunity purely because of where he currently plays would make little sense.

7 Best Miniseries You Can Binge in a Weekend, Ranked

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Feel free to connect with him or check out his work. He's everywhere — Upwork, YouTube, Spotify, SoundCloud, Collider, LinkedIn, Instagram.

Committing to a new TV show can feel like signing up for a part-time job. Some series run for years, and by the time viewers catch up, they've forgotten why they started in the first place. Miniseries offer a way out of that rut. They tell self-contained stories with a clear beginning and end, and the best of them don't waste a minute of their short runtime.

Of course, that alone doesn't make a show worth binge-watching. The ones that stick are the ones that make every episode feel essential and leave the audience itching to watch the next one immediately. With that said, here are six near-perfect miniseries you can catch up on in a weekend and feel completely satisfied.

The Queen's Gambit turned millions into chess fans with no prior understanding of the game, and that is a feat in itself. The Netflix series follows Beth Harmon (Anya Taylor-Joy), an orphan in 1950s Kentucky who learns to play chess from the janitor at her orphanage, Mr. Shaibel (Bill Camp). It doesn't take long for Beth to prove that she is a prodigy, and as she grows up, she rises through the ranks of the male-dominated chess world. Her goal is to beat the best player in the world, Soviet champion Vasily Borgov (Marcin Dorociński), but her growing dependence on pills and alcohol threatens to get in the way.

The Queen's Gambit is a masterclass in adrenaline because it shoots every match like a boxing fight. The show makes it impossible to look away as Beth plans her next move while the camera captures every nervous glance and shift in confidence across the board. Even viewers who couldn't tell a rook from a bishop can understand exactly what's at stake. Underneath all that, though, the series gives equal weight to Beth’s personal struggles, which adds more meaning to every game she plays. The Queen’s Gambit makes the protagonist her own greatest opponent, and in doing so, delivers one of the most satisfying finales of all time.

Station Eleven is a post-apocalyptic story that is more interested in hope than survival. The HBO series, based on Emily St. John Mandel's novel, begins on the night a deadly flu pandemic sweeps across the world. Twenty years later, Kirsten Raymonde (Mackenzie Davis) travels around the Great Lakes region with the Traveling Symphony, a troupe of actors and musicians who perform Shakespeare for the scattered communities that are left. The story jumps between timelines and slowly reveals how Kirsten's past is connected to a famous actor named Arthur Leander (Gael García Bernal) and a mysterious comic book called “Station Eleven.”

Most stories about the end of the world are generally predictable, but Station Eleven takes a surprisingly different approach. Instead of focusing on violent survivors fighting over dwindling supplies, it explores what people hold on to when everything they know is gone. Shakespeare, music, and storytelling might seem pointless in a world struggling to rebuild, but they give its survivors something worth living for. The shifting timelines also work beautifully because they reveal connections between characters who may never have met but whose lives were shaped by the same people and events. Station Eleven is a show that can make a grown adult cry. Ten episodes is a slightly bigger commitment, but by the end, one starts to appreciate how the series is essentially one big love letter to art.

Dopesick tells the fictionalized story of how America's opioid crisis began, and it doesn't hold back in any way. The Hulu series follows small-town doctor Dr. Samuel Finnix (Michael Keaton) in a Virginia mining community who is persuaded to prescribe a new painkiller called OxyContin to his patients. Among them is Betsy Mallum (Kaitlyn Dever), a young coal miner who gets hurt on the job. At the same time, the story follows the investigators who spend years trying to hold those responsible accountable. Dopesick is not an easy watch, and it isn't supposed to be. It's one thing to read about the opioid crisis in the news and another to watch a bright, young character like Betsy turn into someone who will do anything for the next pill.

The show is especially infuriating in how it contrasts the suffering of ordinary Americans with the calculated decisions being made by those in power. Michael Keaton delivers one of his finest performances as a well-meaning doctor who gradually realizes that he has helped destroy the very community he wanted to protect. The shifting timelines can occasionally be difficult to follow, but they allow the audience to see how a crisis of this scale developed over years of misleading marketing and corporate greed. Dopesick dramatizes characters and events, but its themes are painfully real.

Band of Brothers is the gold standard for war dramas, even over two decades after its release. The miniseries, produced by Steven Spielberg and Tom Hanks, follows Easy Company, a unit of American paratroopers during World War II. The story begins with their grueling training in Georgia and follows them from D-Day all the way to the end of the war in Europe. Band of Brothers is far from any other war story, though. Even with its epic scale, it’s fascinating how intimate and personal the show feels.

Part of that is because every episode of Band of Brothers opens with short interviews of real Easy Company veterans, and by the end of the series, the audience comes to know these men as more than just action heroes. They become familiar faces, each with their own personality, fears, and reasons for fighting. That makes the show's battle sequences all the more devastating, because every casualty feels like the loss of someone the audience has spent hours getting to know. The series also refuses to romanticize combat. And while Damian Lewis is excellent as Major Richard Winters, the real strength of Band of Brothers lies in its ensemble cast. Ten episodes make for a heavy weekend, but Band of Brothers is the rare show that feels like an honor to watch.

One Day is the kind of love story that sneaks up on its audience. The series, based on David Nicholls' novel, begins in 1988, when aspiring writer Emma Morley (Ambika Mod) and privileged party boy Dexter Mayhew (Leo Woodall) spend one night together after their graduation in Edinburgh. Each episode then checks in on them on the same day, July 15, over the next two decades, as life keeps pulling them in very different directions.

There's something painfully relatable about watching Emma and Dexter miss each other year after year while also not being able to communicate their feelings to each other. One Day captures the way people drift apart in their twenties and the way some people always seem to find their way back. Mod and Leo share an effortless chemistry that keeps the audience coming back for more. Keep the tissues close, though, because One Day has an ending that will wreck even the toughest of viewers.

Sharp Objects is a slow, haunting thriller that is sure to get under anyone’s skin. The miniseries, based on Gillian Flynn's novel, stars Amy Adams as Camille Preaker, a troubled journalist who returns to her small hometown of Wind Gap, Missouri, to report on the murders of two young girls. However, going home means facing her cold, controlling mother, Adora (Patricia Clarkson), and her mysterious teenage half-sister, Amma (Eliza Scanlen). Gradually, the audience realizes that the deeper Camille digs into the case, the more she has to confront her own dark past.

Sharp Objects unfolds with suffocating patience, and the confined, small-town setting of Wind Gap only contributes to that feeling. Camille’s memories of her past are fragmented, so at any given point, the audience only knows just as much as she remembers. Much of the show’s brilliance comes from Adams’s brilliant performance as a woman who has spent her whole life trying to run away from her trauma. Clarkson’s Adora, meanwhile, is terrifying in an entirely different way. Their deeply dysfunctional relationship is what gives the series so much of its psychological weight. The real masterpiece is the finale, though, which arrives so suddenly that one has to immediately rewind to make sure what they just saw. Sharp Objects is an eight-episode-long gut punch.

The Night Of is the most gripping crime drama HBO has ever made — one that deserves to be appreciated for years to come. The story follows Nasir "Naz" Khan (Riz Ahmed), a Pakistani-American college student from Queens who borrows his father's cab to go to a party in Manhattan. Instead, he ends up spending the night with a mysterious young woman, and when he wakes up, she has been brutally murdered. Naz can't remember what happened, and with all the evidence pointing to him, he soon finds himself on trial. His only hope is John Stone (John Turturro), a low-rent lawyer who sees something in Naz that no one else does.

The Night Of is brilliant in how it captures the slow horror of the justice system. Watching Naz go from a terrified, soft-spoken kid to a man hardened by the brutal realities of prison is genuinely heartbreaking, and Ahmed makes every step of that change feel believable. At Rikers, Naz starts making compromises that would have been unthinkable to him before his arrest, and the audience can’t help but sympathize with him for it. What makes The Night Of so devastating is that even if Naz manages to walk free, there is no returning to the person he was before that night. At the end, the question of whether Naz did it or not almost becomes secondary to the way his life has changed forever. The Night Of doesn't hand out easy answers, and that's exactly why it's the best miniseries on this list.

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

Kratos Sales Up 36%, Free Cash Flow Negative: Rapid Earnings Reaction

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Defense demand keeps building at Kratos, with backlog at a record. We look at how profit and cash flow look once the adjustments are set aside.

Kratos Defense & Security Solutions (KTOS -7.15%) grew third-quarter revenue 36%, or 23% excluding acquisitions, to $471.8 million, and new orders ran 2.2 times sales. Shares slipped about 0.6% in after-hours trading. Demand is strong, but Kratos is still consuming cash to grow. It used $29.4 million of free cash in the quarter and expects to use $110 million to $120 million this year.

Gross profit grew about as fast as sales, so the GAAP loss comes from costs below that line. Selling and administrative costs rose 45%, and amortization of acquired intangibles jumped after recent acquisitions.

Adjusted EBITDA rose while GAAP operating income fell. The gap is add-backs, and the biggest jump was amortization of capitalized contract and development costs, up tenfold.

Stock sales, not operations, are funding the expansion. Kratos raised $1.35 billion that way in nine months, and the release defers 2027 cash flow guidance until late February.

Watch whether the 2027 forecast holds if the continuing resolution runs past March 31, which the release says would force a reassessment. Also look for 2027 operating and free cash flow guidance in late February.

Swivel Wants To Create Agentic Infrastructure For CTV

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Agentic media buying is the No. 1 topic du jour in ad tech discussion circles. But how does agentic buying even work when it comes to buying TV and streaming ads? Those channels remain more old school and anchored by direct human relationships, at least compared with web display advertising.

TV is a media channel that’s going to have its own particular setup and adoption curve.

At least that’s the thesis behind Swivel, one such agentic ad-buying startup.

Swivel is a publisher tech startup founded by ad tech entrepreneurs and investors: the former FreeWheeler Frans Vermeulen and SpringServe co-founders Matt Dearborn, Rich Lin and Joseph Hirsch. The startup aims to make a name for itself as a company that can automate the manual tasks in publisher ad operations, from campaign optimization to pricing adjustments.

In other words, Swivel wants to be an “end-to-end system” of agents that handles all aspects of campaign management, from media planning to optimization and reporting, Hirsch told Streamlined. The eventual goal, he said, is for an agentic infrastructure that spans the CTV advertising chain in particular and is part of media sellers’ yield optimization.

AI product adoption may seem inevitable. But Hirsch acknowledged that there is no cakewalk in developing agentic solutions for every step in the CTV chain and to gain wide adoption.

Initially, Swivel was “very active in the middle to tail end of the agentic media workflow,” Hirsch said, especially where yield optimization is concerned. But now, he said, “we’re much more active at the front end of the agentic workflow,” referring to campaign functions before any media is purchased, such as planning, forecasting and ad ops rule-setting for guaranteed deals.

Rather than a single consolidated product, Swivel has multiple agents each responsible for parts of a campaign buy, such as budgeting, bid trafficking, pacing, yield management and post-campaign reporting, just to name a few, Hirsch said.

Which is certainly not to say that all publishers are comfortable entrusting their entire media-selling process to AI. Many publishers are interested in using one or two agents, “so as not to disrupt their entire ad ops workflow,” he said. It’s common for clients to pick and choose what they would prefer to outsource to the AI model.

Plus, agentic automation isn’t necessarily simplification. There is a real concern that agentic platforms and MCPs just become another tangle in the fragmentation of CTV media. Plus, with every vendor evolving into an AI solution of some sort, CTV advertisers and ad sellers might easily feel like there are too many agents in the kitchen. (Had to.)

Hirsch said this type of fragmentation is addressed in part by specifications within the Ad Context Protocol (AdCP), a set of standardized protocols for online ad supply chain agents to communicate and negotiate. AdCP includes a specification called adagents.json that allows publishers to declare which agents are authorized to sell their inventory, not unlike sellers.json, Hirsch said. (Editor’s note: The AdCP is collaborative with Prebid and somewhat in opposition to the IAB Tech Lab’s AAMP and ARTF frameworks.)

Agentic media buying on TV screens faces a long, uphill climb, even with optimistic expectations. But there are real perks to incentivize adoption by CTV advertisers and sellers. And those benefits are distinct even from programmatic.

For example, certain non-standard CTV ad formats – such as sponsorship placements, home screen ads and pause ads – can differ across streaming TV apps, smart TV models and CTV buying channels. And without ad standardization across publishers, these CTV buys are difficult to set up programmatically, Hirsch said.

Ad tech vendors and industry orgs have recently announced standardized ways to integrate home screen and pause ads. But it takes time to build wider buy-in.

With agentic media buying, on the other hand, it’s possible to automate ad sales for formats that are unavailable or difficult to buy programmatically, Hirsch said. Along with nontraditional ad formats, he said, sequential media (having creatives appear in a certain order within a program) is another type of buying strategy that can be managed agentically but not programmatically.

Today, “our focus is still 100% CTV,” Hirsch said, but the startup intends to expand into the retail media space, which is especially promising because of the rising overlap between retail media and CTV, he said. Just look at Walmart, Vizio and Vibe.co.

AppLovin is growing at a blistering pace, but Wall Street investors remain cautious about the business.

High-end ad blockers mimic some of the same behavior as bot traffic. So WiseGuyGaming repurposed the tools it was already using to identify bots to reveal its ad-blocking audience as well.

Criteo has weathered considerable storms in its day, including a painful moment in March 2020 when its market cap bottomed out below $500 million. Can it pull off yet another turnaround?

Disaffection and outright anger toward US sports institutions run deeper than logo-cluttered fields. Leagues and teams are sacrificing player safety and fan enjoyment for marketing dollars.

People Inc.’s referral traffic from Google search is down 70% over the past two years, and by 26% in Q3 alone. But its digital revenue is still growing, albeit slowly.

On Tuesday, Chord, a six-year-old commerce tech startup, acquired Zenlytic, an AI startup with an anthropomorphized data analyst agent named “Zoë,” for $5 million.

AdExchanger is where marketers, agencies, publishers and tech companies go for the latest information on the trends that are transforming digital media and marketing, from data, privacy, identity and AI to commerce, CTV, measurement and mobile.

Rebuilding After Wildfires Means Rethinking Residential Design

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

The SCI-Arc Resilient Futures Task Force was created to rethink standard rebuilding methods after the January 2025 fires in Los Angeles exposed deep weaknesses in Southern California's default residential standards. Bringing together teachers, researchers, and students, the initiative gives displaced homeowners direct, practical help with building safety, soil toxicity, emergency funding, and insurance claims through public community meetings.

The SCI-Arc Resilient Futures Task Force was created to rethink standard rebuilding methods after the January 2025 fires in Los Angeles exposed deep weaknesses in Southern California's default residential standards. Bringing together teachers, researchers, and students, the initiative gives displaced homeowners direct, practical help with building safety, soil toxicity, emergency funding, and insurance claims through public community meetings.

Nearly two years later, residents are still rebuilding their lives as wildfires become more frequent and severe. Instead of repeating past mistakes caused by outdated safety codes, standard construction habits, and insurance gaps, this crisis presents a critical moment to create safer, long-term architectural designs in fire-prone areas.

Nearly two years later, residents are still rebuilding their lives as wildfires become more frequent and severe. Instead of repeating past mistakes caused by outdated safety codes, standard construction habits, and insurance gaps, this crisis presents a critical moment to create safer, long-term architectural designs in fire-prone areas.

SCI-Arc Harnesses Global Expertise for Resilient Building

Grounded in the institution's tradition of critical design research, the Resilient Futures Task Force connects academic expertise directly with real-world disaster recovery. The coalition brings together an international network of architects, structural engineers, scientists, material specialists, non-profit developers, and financial strategists. Key collaborators and participating institutions include Code Red Consultants, Angel City Lumber, SGH, JPMorgan Chase, Century Housing, Clearinghouse CDFI, Builders FirstSource, Plant Prefab, Sexsmith Architects, Timberlab, ReSAWN, Urban Machine, and TU Delft.

Extending its focus from temporary aid to permanent resilience, the Task Force organized public panels on alternative building materials, mass timber construction, rebuilding economics, and fire-adapted landscaping to help communities make informed, long-term architectural decisions.

As the largest collection of Wildland-Urban Interface (WUI) architectural proposals to date, the showcase featured ongoing work from prominent practices including Lorcan O'Herlihy, SPF, Studio Murnane, Morphosis, Heather Flood and Story Dimson Studio, Knowhow Shop, and Scott Uriu.

The Task Force is publishing a comprehensive book documenting these projects, creating a permanent reference guide for building in high-risk environments. Next, SCI-Arc plans to expand its research through institutional partnerships that combine artificial intelligence, environmental risk modeling, and advanced simulation to shape resilient building standards across the region.

India as an AI hedge? The country's stocks could see robust inflows as markets globally tank

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

The Indian stock market is emerging as an attractive hedge as investors seek refuge from a brutal sell-off in the artificial intelligence trade, making it a strong contender for global capital.

As sentiment around AI sours, foreign investors, who have been underweight on the Indian market for nearly two years due to high valuations, subdued earnings growth and lack of local AI tech champions, could start returning.

"If these foreign long only investor groups were to neutralize their underweight in Indian equities, this could translate into potential net inflows of around $115 billion," Rajiv Batra, head of Asia and co-head of global emerging markets equity strategy at JPMorgan, told CNBC.

He said that multiple factors are currently stacked in favor of the country, such as falling global oil prices, a drop in U.S. Treasury yields, a recovery in earnings growth of Indian companies and unwinding of global AI trade.

"India has emerged as an 'AI hedge' for portfolios trimming or diversifying out of crowded AI trades," Batra said.

Over the last month, the country's benchmark Nifty 50 has gained nearly 9%, outperforming global markets with heavy AI exposure, including the U.S., Japan, South Korea and Taiwan that are deep in the red as investors ditch tech names.

On Friday, as major Asian markets tracked a sell-off on Wall Street overnight, India's benchmark indexes bucked the trend to trade higher. That divergence becomes stark as Japan's Nikkei 225 and Taiwan's Taiex entered bear territory, while South Korea's Kospi deepened its bear market and the tech-heavy Nasdaq Composite entered into correction territory overnight.

"India has been dubbed sort of the anti-AI trade, and our view there is that the underlying economic fundamentals have actually been better," Timothy Moe, chief Asia-Pacific equity strategist and co-head of Asia macro research at Goldman Sachs, told CNBC's "Squawk Box Asia" on Friday.

He added that markets like India and Australia that were less exposed to the AI trade will see "some relative outperformance," adding that India's "domestic demand story" gives it an edge between the two.

Unlike Korea and Taiwan, where indexes are heavily concentrated in AI hardware stocks, Indian benchmarks have little exposure to this segment and are instead focused on financial services, consumption, energy, industrials and other services companies.

"India becomes an attractive alternative because of its size, liquidity and relatively lower dependence on the AI cycle," at a time when global investors want to cut exposure to AI-related stocks, Feroze Azeez, joint chief executive at Anand Rathi Wealth, told CNBC.

Foreign investors, which net sold more than $31 billion until October of this year, have turned net buyers over the past four days, investing over $2.3 billion, according to data from NSDL.

This wave of optimism around the Indian markets comes at a time when corporate earnings for the September quarter have shown material improvement.

As per data shared by JPMorgan, earnings growth of listed Indian companies is tracking at more than 20%, and consensus earnings per share estimates have been revised up 0.5% for 2026 and 0.6% for 2027 over the past month.

But despite a steep underperformance of Indian indexes over the last year — even for this year so far the key benchmarks are in the red — and the improvement in earnings growth, its valuations are still relatively higher than other emerging markets.

Global brokerage Bernstein, however, disagrees that the South Asian country should be viewed as an AI hedge.

"India is increasingly being pitched as the hedge to the global AI trade. We disagree. India is neither an AI beneficiary nor an anti-AI trade," it said in a report on Monday, arguing that India's valuations remain expensive.

India is trading at a rolling 12-month forward price-to-earnings of 18.6 times, higher than China's 9.3x and Brazil's 7.9x, the report showed. It is also trading at a premium to AI-heavy markets like South Korea and Taiwan.

"There are easier and cheaper ways to hedge AI than investing in India," Saurabh Mukherjea, founder and chief investment officer of Marcellus Investment Managers, told CNBC. He added, though, that India's earnings growth has been "stellar" for the last two quarters and the valuation of some "quality large caps" is at a decade low.

India has always traded at a premium to the emerging markets of 1.4 times on average since 2007 due to its "durable earnings and a domestically driven growth engine," JPMorgan's Batra said.

The country's markets have struggled since late 2024 due to slowing earnings growth, but that has changed over the last two quarters.

India's near-term appeal was diluted by "high crude, a weak rupee and the availability of high-growth opportunities elsewhere," Hari Shyamsunder, vice president and senior institutional portfolio manager of India equities at Templeton Global Investments, told CNBC.

He pointed out that Nifty now trades below its "10-year average forward P/E," while MSCI India, in October, was at its lowest forward multiple since 2020.

"If crude softens sustainably and growth faces headwinds elsewhere, the picture could change quickly," he said, adding that foreign capital inflows would depend on sustained earnings growth over the next few quarters.

Live: Singapore Airlines Unveils All-New First & Business Class Suites For Its Boeing 777-9

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Last Updated: 12:55 AM EST Nov 5 2026 by Tom Boon

Singapore Airlines has unveiled the next stage in its total refresh of the passenger experience. Today, the Asian airline revealed its new first-class and business-class seats to the world at an event held at the Marina Bay Sands Exhibition Centre in Singapore. The airline will spend more than $2bn SGD ($1.5bn) on the upgrade program.

Airlines around the world are constantly looking to innovate when it comes to passenger experience. With a trophy cabinet of awards, including 2026 SkyTrax Best Airline, Singapore Airlines is looking at the experience in more detail than some others. A cabin refresh that was initially planned to launch with the arrival of the Boeing 777-9 is now expected from H2 next year and will also apply to some of the airline’s Airbus A350-900s.

Singapore Airlines has confirmed that the new Boeing 777-9s will all be delivered with the new cabin experience. Additionally, 41 Airbus A350-900s will get the new cabin. The A350-900 long-haul and A350-900ULR aircraft will get the cabin. However, the A350-900 mid-range aircraft, Boeing 777-300s, Airbus A380s, and all other fleets will not receive the retrofit.

Singapore Airlines CEO, Goh Choon Phong, is welcoming the media and invited guests to Singapore ahead of the reveal of the new Singapore Airlines long-haul and ultra-long-haul cabins. He confirmed that Singapore Airlines will be bringing first class to the world's longest flight between Singapore and New York.

"The A350 ultra-long-range aircraft will also feature the new first-class cabin, and this will be the first time we're featuring first-class on the ultra-long-range planes. So, when you take our flight between Singapore and New York in the future, you will also have the option of going in a first-class cabin."

Singapore Airlines has taken a bold approach with its new business class cabin, designed in collaboration with BMW’s Design Works and RECARO. The new seats are incredibly well thought out from a passenger experience. However, the colour scheme is a departure from the usual Batik-inspired palette that the airline has become famous for.

When describing the new cabin, one of the features consistently pointed out by the Chief Commercial Officer, Lee Lik Hsin, was the fact that passengers sit and sleep facing forward. Work, sleep, dining, and relaxing have all been considered. The seat, for example, has a dedicated space to place your laptop during the meal service, a huge bugbear for many passengers during the meal service. Additionally, the airline will also be introducing doors to its business class seats for the first time.

Arguably the biggest upgrade that Boeing 777 and Airbus A350 passengers will see is the introduction of double beds in select business class seats. According to Singapore Airlines, the full-flat beds measure 79 inches in length and up to 24 inches in width, while a padded bedsheet with memory foam will provide "additional cushioning and support".

When it comes to first class, Singapore Airlines has another bespoke product. Designed with PriestmanGoode, each first class cabin will have six seats, each with a floor to ceiling enclosure. Unlike the business class, the first class cabin sticks to the established Singapore Airlines design language.

The highlight of the first class cabin is the two center seats. These can be combined to make a sofa or double bed. The seats can be synchronised and adjusted as one. Of course, if the passengers occupying these seats are unknown to each other, a full height wall is able to separate them.

Singapore Airlines says that combined, the center seats offer “The largest double bed in commercial aviation”, measuring 80 inches in length and 60 inches in width. Another particular highlight of the seat, built by Collins Aerospace, is the option for face to face dining at all seats, allowing up to four people to dine together in the center seats.

AOI Sales Up 127%, Gross Margin Stuck at 28%: Rapid Earnings Reaction

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Datacenter demand is lifting sales at this optical networking company, but profit is not keeping up. We look at what supports the bottom line and what the outlook requires.

Applied Optoelectronics (AAOI -20.89%) grew third-quarter revenue 127% to $269.9 million, led by datacenter sales. Shares fell about 8% in after-hours trading. Profit is not keeping pace with sales. Gross margin is stuck near 28%, operations still lost money, and the GAAP profit came from other income.

GAAP net income rests on $10.1 million of other income, because operations lost money. The largest piece, a line called "other income (expense), net," gets no explanation in the release.

Gross margin barely moved even as revenue more than doubled. Ramping new transceiver lines usually costs yield, which holds margin down until volumes settle. Guidance for next quarter is 29.5% to 31% on a non-GAAP basis, only a modest step up.

Guidance calls for fourth-quarter revenue about 49% above this quarter at the midpoint. The company also says its 2027 US capacity expansion will take longer, because it is waiting on electrical power and facing other construction issues, though it kept its end-of-2027 target.

Watch whether GAAP operating income turns positive in the fourth quarter as revenue rises toward the guided range. Also track whether the US expansion gets its electrical power in time for the end-of-2027 capacity target.

The Wheel Of Time Universe's True Finale Officially Arrives June 2027

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Wheel of Time fans are still uncertain about the franchise's live-action future after how things played out for the Amazon show. Unfortunately, the series didn't manage to adapt the books well enough to win over Robert Jordan fans, and viewership numbers simply didn't warrant the show's massive budget.

As such, we never got to see how the story would've played out on screen, and certainly never came close to seeing an adaptation of the Wheel of Time's epic conclusion, The Last Battle (or Tarmon Gai'don in the Old Tongue). A proper live-action adaptation of Jordan's complete saga may never happen, though there's renewed hope thanks to iwot Studios' upcoming animated projects, including a new series from Arcane producer Thomas Vu.

Fortunately, fans will finally have the opportunity to experience Wheel of Time's true epic finale for themselves in 2027, thanks to another major release that's already generated serious buzz.

Although Amazon's The Wheel of Time was canceled after just three seasons last year, fans will finally get the opportunity to experience the story's epic conclusion in 2027 with the release of War of the Dragon: The Wheel of Time. Developed by Dire Wolf, the upcoming strategy board game is the first officially licensed tabletop adaptation of Robert Jordan's fantasy saga, drawing from all 14 books and bringing The Last Battle to life in a way the television series never managed to accomplish.

Unlike the Amazon adaptation, which ended long before Rand al'Thor's final confrontation with the Dark One, War of the Dragon covers the entire conflict between the Light and Shadow, culminating in the events of A Memory of Light. Players will take control of opposing factions, recruit familiar characters, command armies, and prepare for Tarmon Gai'don.

The game features two distinct ways to play, including a beginner-friendly Hero Mode and a more expansive Epic Mode. While Hero Mode focuses on the preparations leading up to the final confrontation, Epic Mode actually allows players to experience The Last Battle itself, potentially changing the ending depending on which side emerges victorious.

The release has already a huge hit ahead of its actual launch, with the Kickstarter campaign ending in June after raising an impressive $1,166,993 from 6,156 backers, easily surpassing its original $50,000 funding goal (though these goals are typically set quite low intentionally).

According to the official Kickstarter, backers can expect their copies to arrive in 2027, with June listed as the estimated delivery date. Dire Wolf has clarified that U.S. fulfillment is expected during the first half of 2027, while international orders may take several additional months. The publisher anticipates all backers receiving their games before the end of the year, although manufacturing and shipping timelines remain subject to change.

Fortunately, fans who missed the Kickstarter campaign aren't entirely out of luck. The standard Dragon Reborn Edition is officially scheduled for a retail release in 2027, available through participating local game stores and international distribution partners. A specific retail launch date hasn't been confirmed, though wider availability is expected to follow the initial Kickstarter fulfillment period.

For Wheel of Time fans disappointed by the television adaptation's premature conclusion, War of the Dragon is giving us something truly exciting, which is the opportunity to finally be a part of The Last Battle and determine the fate of Jordan's world for ourselves.

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →
