Japanese Rural Life Adventure now available for PS4

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Publisher G-MODE Corporation and developer GAME START LLC have released a PlayStation 4 version of slow life simulation game Japanese Rural Life Adventure via PlayStation Store for $19.99 / £15.49 / €18.99 / ¥2,750, the companies announced. PlayStation Plus users can purchase it at a 20 percent-off discount price of $15.99 / £12.39 / €15.19 / ¥2,200 until November 20.

Japanese Rural Life Adventure first launched for Apple Arcade on September 15, 2023, followed by Switch and PC via Steam on March 3, 2026.

Here is an overview of the game, via G-MODE Corporation:

A relaxing slow-life simulation game where you can experience the charm of traditional Japanese countryside living, all in a beautifully crafted pixel-art world.

Japanese Rural Life Adventure is a relaxing slow-life simulation where you can experience the charm of traditional Japanese countryside living, all in a beautifully crafted pixel-art world.

Live in the Japanese Countryside Surrounded by Nature

Fix up an old house, make the yard nice, grow your own crops, and become self-sufficient.

View the cherry blossoms in the spring; attend the festival in the summer; eat roasted sweet potatoes in the fall; hear the temple bell ring in winter, and then it’s time for the first shrine visit of the New Year.

Rice—the essential source of energy for the people in Japan. Salt, soy sauce, miso—seasonings no Japanese kitchen can do without. In this countryside, many things city dwellers take for granted must be made by hand.

By standing in the kitchen or cooking around the traditional irori hearth of your heritage home, you can simulate preparing a wide variety of dishes and seasonings. Food made through your own effort tastes extra special.

Don’t be surprised if the game makes you hungry!

Bring Back Life to the Village Through Various Events

Many events unfold depending on the season, location, and time of day. A villager might ask you to repair an old waterwheel, or to help reopen a shuttered candy shop.

Strengthen your relationships with the locals and restore the liveliness the village once had. As you complete requests and interact with the villagers, you may earn useful items or unlock new areas to explore.

Your journey begins as the protagonist, a newcomer from the city, starts a solitary life in an old countryside house.

Start by clearing the overgrown weeds, cleaning the floors, and repairing the “shoji” paper screens.

By the time you’ve completed the tutorial, you’ll have learned the basics of village life.

Each morning, an elderly woman visits to sell daily necessities, and each afternoon, a man arrives to buy items you no longer need. Buy and sell goods as you steadily gather everything required to thrive in the village and complete your missions.

Digiday Media Buying Summit October Recap: Assessing the ups and challenges of AI’s spread across the media agency landscape

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

This article is part of Digiday’s coverage of its Digiday Media Buying Summit. More from the series →

Artificial intelligence has in short order swept across all facets of the media agency world, from internal tools that save time and improve efficiency to planning and measurement tools that dig deeper into oceans of data to identify audiences for their clients, to even creative production that works at geometric levels of produtivity.

But agencies are also called upon to offer consultative guidance to clients on how the latter can put AI to use. The crush of effort has resulted in new priorities and pressures at both independent and holding company.

Each Digiday event kicks off with the edit team breaking down current industry themes. Here’s where we think we are:

AI is inevitable in its influence but also its impact. Whether it’s agentic buying or planning, AEO/GEO or guiding clients through ways to understand and implement AI, it’s changing processes and compressing timelines.

But warning signs are everywhere, from ensuring human oversight of anything to do with client budgets to reining in token usage or insufficient ROI. AI will factor in some way with everything we discuss these next few days.

The drive toward lower funnel, performance results has dragged a spotlight onto the hidden fees that agencies often struggle with or sweep under the rug. How can transparency sit alongside better results?

There’s no stopping this train, as more and more brands opt to devote a larger share of marketing dollars to the field. When is it too much? And how to ensure performance is still delivered.

If you’re a holding company agency, principal media is a reality in some part of your business. But are there ways to be part of it without feeling you sold your soul?

Case for and against: principal mediaDMBS kicked off with an interactive session that invited a debate on principal media, then asked the audience to vote on which side won the debate — since it’s the most widely debated topic in the media agency world. Principal media puts agencies in the position of buying and selling inventory for and to their clients. To its detractors, including Kamran Asghar, CEO of Crossmedia, who argued against, it has corrupted the meaning of “agency” by putting agencies on both sides of the investment equation.

But the argument can be made — as it was by Forrester’s vp and senior agency analyst Jay Pattisall — that it’s here to stay for those agencies with the scale to pull it off, so long as there can be sufficient transparency for the client in the process. The final vote: 72% opposed principal media.

The new agency talent equationAI and emerging tools are making it possible to build ideas that would have been impossible just a few years ago. M+C Saatchi CEO Nadja Bellan-White explored what this convergence means for agency leaders — from how creative and media teams need to work together to the new kinds of talent, skills and perspectives agencies need to hire to meet the demands of clients today. The key: balancing human talent and the temptation of technology, most acutely AI.

Injecting more transparency and certainty in mediaIn modern media investment, too many brands are playing roulette — relying on blind platform algorithms, chasing self-reported metrics and reacting to phantom signals that lead straight to knee-jerk budget pivots. Tack on ecosystems cluttered with opacity, hidden fees and black-box attribution. Feels like the deck’s stacked against media specialists, right? Devin Bellamy of sports gaming disruptor Underdog and Eden Collective founder and CEO Alison Monk engaged in a no-BS look at the radical media rigor and intellectual humility needed to outsmart outspenders.

The creator authenticity problemCreators have become a powerful part of the media mix, but as the business matures, authenticity is getting harder to define — and even harder to protect. A session featuring Alex Mathieu head of creative strategy & vp at creator agency Open Influence, explored what makes creator partnerships actually feel credible, where brands and agencies get it wrong and how marketers can balance scale, performance and genuine audience connection. As Mathieu said:

When media meets creative techThe walls between media, creative, data and technology are getting more porous by the day, especially as AI cuts across them all by dint of its value. How far into the creative and design process should media agencies go? And which AI tools and platforms are actually helping agencies plan, build and buy more effectively, rather than simply adding another layer of tech? Laurel Burton, CEO of Instrument and Carly Gomez, Crocs’ CMO, walked the audience through their website, social media and e-commerce efforts this year, and how technology sped up the process while digging deeper insights.

Retail media’s shakeoutThe retail media gold rush created an increasingly crowded market. Now the question is who survives. Are advertisers finally ready to cull the long tail of lower-value networks, and what separates the retail media businesses that become essential from those that become expendable? Dentsu’s new head of commerce Aisha Khan addressed these issues, concluding that while some consolidation is inevitable, growth in this sector will continue to grow because it offers media agencies new opportunities to harness closed-loop measurement in a way that benefits both upper- and lower-funnel marketing efforts. Khan also believes in-store will be the next area that’s ripe for growth and expansion:

Do you know what AI is costing your agency?

Agencies and clients across the industry are realizing finally that AI gains mean little if you can’t fathom the real operating costs associated with AI systems or agentic tools. Christina Riccitelli, chief innovation & analytics officer, OpAd Media, and Tony Russo, vp AI innovation & strategy, Mile Marker, represented two agencies with different philosophies on the tech, as they attempt to balance the benefits of AI with a close understanding of its actual cost.

Redesigning your teams around AIAgencies across the globe are investing in AI applications in pursuit of productivity gains. Few have thought hard about how to bed in their new capabilities and operationalize the tech. Gila Wilensky, evp and head of data and media, North America at Monks, walked the audience through how the company has rethought staff structures and client-facing teams to fully take advantage of AI:

Go Fish’s approach to agentic planning and buyingAgentic media planning and buying tools have become an area of intense focus for many agencies and clients. Beyond the hype, however, there are decisions to be made over what agents are actually best placed to do, and how much control they require to operate properly. David Dweck, president of Go Fish Digital, called out the opportunities to apply agentic tools while also pointing to areas where it should be kept away:

Making outcomes-based relationships workOutcome or performance-based client relationships have emerged as a response to pressures facing the agency business model. But while the idea can be theoretically attractive, hashing out an outcome-based arrangement with a client can be much harder in practice. Morgan Scarnato, chief strategy officer of CourtAvenue Media, broke down how to construct an outcomes-based relationship that works for both agency and client:

“I tell our staff, ‘Do your resume every six months, and if you’re not learning stuff here, advancing your skill sets, you should quit and go work someplace else.’”

“I’ve actually been surprised by how much AI content [there is at] a higher strategy level … places like the VPs and the C-suite. I know they know how to do these things. I don’t think they want to take the time to do it. Because this is much easier.”

“It comes down to the brief. I’ve seen some of the worst briefs I’ve ever seen in my career recently, and it’s because they’re so disjointed and there’s no through line on what the not only the marketing goal is as well as the business goal … I think AI is making it worse.”

“Yeah, briefs have definitely gotten worse, and they’re also like jam packed with a lot of things that feel like they start to blur together the swim lanes between what media’s job is versus a creative job or a PR driven job.”

“There’s two forks in the road for the world of agencies. One fork is, ‘I’m going to fully automate everything and eliminate as many humans and costs as possible.’ The other fork in the road is, ‘I’m going to create efficiency and enable the humans I already have, and lean into expertise and make those experts more efficient at what they do, so they can deliver more value for clients.’ So, if I was working at an organization that’s going for full automation and limiting humans, I would be scared. I would argue that’s not the right strategy.”

“So if we’re not pushing the staff to learn new things and keep the sort of ship afloat, you’re going to have these issues where an AI bot spends $10 million versus $10,000 in a DSP, and it’s going to be like, ‘I don’t know what to do’.”

AI remains a real question mark on the future of media and marketing. There are so many ways it can help — and agencies are putting it to work. But there are risks for companies cultivating an over-reliance on this technology, from corporate slop to the expanding sea of sameness.

Bottom line: Humans need to remain at the center to make gains and prevent potentially catastrophic mistakes.

Clients can be great partners but that relationship is under pressure to the point of counterproductive behavior and not speaking the same language. That’s causing undue frustration and putting pressure on agencies trying to divine what clients want and need. AI is making that worse.

Bottom line: Find paths to better and more open communication.

In the age of principal media, transparency is the key to success, but a lack of it is getting in the way of agency/client trust. Pressure to make money at holdcos is impacting the way media and marketing is unfolding, and it affects all parties.

Bottom line: Remove black boxes from the equation.

AI implementation is altering the day-to-day work of agency staffers. It’s an open field for agencies reimagine what talent means as the industry transforms at the speed of AI. How you staff up is for grabs.

Bottom line: There are no clear answers today how that is accomplished, but experimentation and testing will yield answers.

There are myriad definitions of outcome-based agency solutions, but more clarity around identity and intent will determine where outcomes land in service to client goals.

Bottom line: Get on the same page with clients on determining the definition and purpose of outcomes.

In the wake of LiveRamp’s acquisition by Publicis, some marketers are auditioning alternatives to the identity graph company.

Now that Publicis has closed its acquisition of LiveRamp, marketers have a decision to make.

Google’s Q3 earnings reveal AI Mode’s impact on search advertising growth, plus ongoing diminishing returns from ad tech.

Future of Marketing Briefing: Creators had a big year. Christmas still went to the celebrities

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Creators are everywhere in marketing these days — everywhere except Christmas ads.

The first batch of festive campaigns, from John Lewis, SharkNinja, Ulta Beauty and Burberry among others, makes little for the industry’s favorite obsession. Despite leaning on creators throughout the rest of the year, marketers aren’t doing it for the holiday crunch. That job still belongs to celebrities.

Sephora has cast Seth Rogen as a man hopelessly out of his depth, rescued by one of the retailer’s Beauty Advisors before he can do any lasting damage. Over at Kay, Saturday Night Live’s Colin Jost is put through that most well-worn of Christmas tropes: the frantic hunt for the perfect present for someone special. Sports Direct roped in Nike to push the cliche a step further. Its ad follows an uncle wandering the aisles in search of something for his niece, blissfully unaware that a squad of Nike athletes is quietly steering him to the right choice. And Gucci has Mariah Carey — the industry version of the relative who gets passed from house to house every December. This year it’s the luxury brand’s turn to host her.

Why these brands do this year after year is no secret. Celebrities, particularly the calibre fronting this year’s crop, are the advertising equivalent of tinsel: nobody calls it innovative but a tree looks bare without it. For CMOs wary of how quickly an ad can set off the internet these days, that familiarity is a safety blanket. John Lewis is the latest case. Within a day of release, its spot had Piers Morgan calling it “possibly the worst advert I’ve ever seen for anything” and Mumsnet testing into the “ungrateful” grandchildren. Dame Kristin Scott Thomas, the ad’s star, escaped without a scratch.

“Part of the reason brands use celebrities in ads is because they’re borrowing fame,” said Lynne Deason, head of creative excellence at Kantar. “Christmas is this mass market moment where brands are trying to reach everyone and so often brands are looking for a celebrity that everyone will recognize rather than maybe a particular audience.”

None of which is a knock on creators. Indeed, marketers have spent the year handing creators budget, stage time and, increasingly, the lead role in global campaigns. Christmas ads, however, play by older rules.  It is the most scrutinized piece of work a brand releases all year, with the biggest budget and the most senior sign-off, and its conventions have barely changed in a decade: a big film, a broad audience and, more often than not, a famous face. Striking to that formula keeps a brand in line with every rival in the ad break. Leaving it puts the brand out on its own and few CMOs want to be the one doing that in December.

“That’s perhaps harder with creators because people tend to tap into them for particular interests,” said Deason. “Obviously there are some who are becoming very famous in their own right with mass audiences but there are fewer of those. And Chrismtas is such a competitive time.”

How long that takes is anyone’s guess. Plenty of creators are household names within their own niche but Christmas needs a face that granny and the teenagers on the sofa will bith recognise. Take Tesco’s Christmas ad starring comedian Roisin Conaty and actor Anna Maxwell Martin. It is currently the most talked about commercial in the U.K, according to Sprout Social. So far, people have posted or talked about the ad 3,263 times (volume) racking up over 36,000 total interactions like likes, comments and shares (engagement). Attitude toward it, however, is divided, with only 51% of conversations positive. This is due, according to the researchers, to people feeling that the advert’s premise is unrealistic and presents a lack of awareness over how retail works. Even so, two famous actors got the country talking, which is more than most festive ads manage.

It’s also why the next few years are likely to bring festive campaigns that put stars and creators side by side. The biggest creators now pull audiences that many TV names would envy and plenty of celebrities have learned to behave like creators – filming their own content and talking straight to fans on their own feeds. SharkNina’s holiday campaign shows how this already works. Kevin Hart, David Beckham, Aryna Siarhiejeŭna and Tom Bradyset up the story by posting short teasers to their social accounts days before the actual spot landed. Laura Dyer, senior vp of global brand and partnerships at SharkNinja, calls the line between the two groups “super fluid.” Before long, the lead in a Christmas ad could to whoever has the most devoted following, whether they made their name on TV, film or a smartphone screen.

Anthropologie certainly has. The retailer handed its holiday campaign to the comedian and creator of the hit viral web series Brooklyn Coffee Shop Pooja Tripathi, who wrote and showran a micro comedy series about a holiday party “from script to shoot”, according to CMO Barbra Sainsurin. The brand stuck to what it knows best, dressing the cast and styling the set. The rollout has trailers and teasers “like a movie launch” and about 50 more creators will spread it across Instagram, TikTok, YouTube, Pinterest and Reddit. The plan, said Sainsurin, is to keep deciding story by story which ones go to creators and which stay with the brand.

“We’re using the holiday to challenge ourselves beyond just the traditional way in which we tell stories and to create a more immersive brand experience through this extension and partnership with an original content creator.”

Kelsey Gray Sherman spent years at WME brokering deals between celebrities and the brands that want them. Last month Lego poached her. She takes up a newly created role as the toymaker’s head of celebrity talent and brand-first partnerships and reports to Jill Wilfert, svp of entertainment, partnerships and content. Lego already knows its way around a box office, having pulled in nearly $700 million from two Lego Movies. A company with that track record hiring an agency dealmaker suggests Lego wants more of its entertainment built around its own brand with famous names and outside partners brought in to amplify.

Over email, Gray Sherman explained what she has planned for the role.

The LEGO Group has a long history of iconic collaborations across its business – from product to entertainment, gaming and more. As those partnerships have grown in recent years, so has the need for dedicated in-house expertise and coordination, which is where this new position was created.

My role will be to connect those dots across a business of talented people already doing fantastic work, and to create a consistent approach to engaging talent while identifying strategic opportunities to extend and elevate relationships across multiple touch points.

What does it say about where you see the future of celebrity talent and brand first partnerships headed? For instance, does celebrity talent include creators now?

The LEGO Group’s invitation to its partners is simple: come create something with us that will help inspire and develop the builders of tomorrow. Looking ahead, the LEGO brand is excited to build and explore new relationships building on this mission, in a way that can extend across different parts of the business and evolve over time.

That absolutely includes creators. The distinction between “traditional celebrity” and creator is increasingly less meaningful to audiences, so the brand’s approach to building and executing relationships with all manner of talent reflects that.

The focus of my role will be on represented talent, while we have several in-house teams that focus specifically on fan creators and local influencers. In collaboration with the brand’s best-in-class social and influencer teams, my role will help bring consistency to those relationships while maximizing the impact and mutual value of each partnership.

How will your  background at WME help here?

My experience across entertainment marketing – and specifically helping grow WME’s brand representation business – brings an expansive perspective on how global brands are engaging top talent and entertainment partners to make an impact in culture.

Beyond providing direct relationships and connectivity, I will be working to help implement best-in-class partnership practices across the organization and streamline how the brand is showing up to industry partners.

What does “work with brands in ways that go beyond traditional licensing and product collaborations” mean for Lego?

It means that product does not always need to be the first point of entry when exploring a partnership. The LEGO Group has an extraordinary history of product innovation with partners, and embracing “brand first partnerships” is not about moving away from that – it is about recognizing the expanding ways two partners can create mutual value for each other and stand-out experiences for fans – with or without product.

“We just need to translate that progress in terms of performance into broader adoption and a larger share of advertiser budgets. I do think AI is a meaningful accelerant there. So as we look into 2027 and beyond, we’re continuing to improve performance with better AI-powered recommendations, and we’re making it easier for advertisers to measure those results using the tools they already rely on, which we know is really important to their confidence in putting more budget behind Snapchat.”

— Snap CEO Evan Spiegel during the company’s Q3 earnings call, when talking about the challenge of turning improvements in ad performance into a larger share of advertiser budgets.

From 13% to 8%: Percentage decrease of paid clicks growth on Google, per earnings

54%: Percentage by which Reddit’s advertising business grew year over year, per earnings

14%: Percentage by which Pinterest’s stock dropped following its Q3 2026 earnings results

Why AI is boosting Meta’s ad business while hurting Google search

Meta’s ad revenue grew 26% last quarter, more than twice as fast as Google Search’s 12%, as AI tools sharpen Meta’s targeting while chatbots like ChatGPT, Claude and Muse threaten Google’s link-clicking model, per CNBC. Both are spending heavily on AI, but Google is far more diversified, and analysts say its stock won’t recover until it proves it can make money from AI-era search.

Meta Hires Rich Latour to Lead In-House Content Studio

Meta has hired former BlackRock content chief and NBC Nightly News veteran Rich Latour to run its in-house content studio, telling the company’s own story through brand, executive and product content as it pushes into AI and smart glasses, according to The Hollywood Reporter.

Apple’s Services growth is slowing as App Store revenue stalls under legal and gaming pressures, and the author argues advertising, not commissions, is its best way to monetize the App Store’s nearly one billion weekly users, per Mobile Dev Memo.

Instagram tries to entice long-form creators

Instagram is luring long-form creators to Features with prominent Explore placement and one-off payments of up to $500,000, but without a YouTube-style payout system, Mosseri admits it hasn’t cracked monetization, risking a repeat of IGTV, according to Social Media Today.

Google search loses its free pass with buyers

With AI Overviews cutting clicks and pushing up costs, media buyers are no longer treating Google Search as untouchable, with clients questioning 10% to 30% of those budgets and shifting some toward Meta, TikTok and AI channels.

In graphic detail: Six thousand brands later, ChatGPT ads still have a conversion problem

The number of brands advertising on ChatGPT has climbed past 6,000, but paid ChatGPT traffic converts at about a third of Google’s rate, so OpenAI still has to prove its ads actually work.

Apple’s Safari block hands WPP a fresh case for InfoSum

WPP says Apple’s Safari crackdown on identity-matching firms has barely affected it, because its InfoSum platform works outside the browser, which strengthens the case for a deal many in the industry once doubted.

Good ads have a new name, ‘entertainment-first’ and SharkNinja is using it for its holiday campaign

SharkNinja’s in-house holiday ad has Kevin Hart, Tom Brady and Aryna Sabalenka wrecking David Beckham’s house, teased with fake CCTV leaks, as the brand bets on “entertainment-first” ads people actually want to share, while still slipping in product demos to drive sales.

OpenAI has ambitious ad revenue targets, which depend on advertiser demand, measurement and monetisation.

Agencies and marketers now frequently use AI tools to create media and creative briefs. But the practice could be a canary in the coalmine for AI overuse in advertising.

The DoorDash-Instacart deal could accelerate commerce media consolidation, promising scale but risking higher prices and lock-in.

By the numbers: Ad tech’s widening divide, as AppLovin surges and rivals struggle in Q3

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

The latest quarterly earnings from six of the publicly traded ad tech companies reveal a tale of two cohorts, with AppLovin and Unity’s substantial growth contrasting with declining revenues at Criteo, Taboola, and Teads.

The companies, which reported their third-quarter results this week, generated markedly different outcomes during the reported period. AppLovin’s revenue increased 55% to $2.19 billion, while Unity posted 32% growth and Magnite reported a double-digit increase. Meanwhile, three of the six experienced annual revenue declines, with Teads suffering the steepest contraction at 26%.

The results also show differences in profitability, with some companies increasing earnings despite lower revenue. Meanwhile, connected TV remained a source of growth for Magnite and Teads, although it was insufficient to offset the latter’s broader decline.

Overall, the numbers illustrate how unevenly the industry’s growth opportunities are being distributed.

A top-line observation is that the two strongest performers are mobile advertising rivals AppLovin and Unity, for some, a trend that underlines the decline of the open web and the resilience of app-based media — both mobile and CTV — companies.

AppLovin reported $2.19 billion in third-quarter revenue, up 55% year over year, alongside net income of $1.44 billion, an increase of 72%.

The company also forecast Q4 revenue of between $2.365 billion and $2.395 billion, and told analysts that its Q3 revenue exceeded the combined revenue of the other five companies, which totaled about $1.94 billion.

Unity, a mobile advertising rival of AppLovin — the two are involved in a recently filed, ongoing legal dispute — reported revenue of $622 million, up 32%, with the Grow Solutions division, i.e., the division that hosts its media offering, generating $457 million, an increase of 44%. The company also reported that its Vector ad platform grew 126% year over year and 40% sequentially during the reporting period.

Elsewhere, in the cohort, growth was more modest, if not outright in marked decline (see chart below).

Magnite reported revenue of $204.2 million, up 14%, with growth primarily driven by its connected TV business. Meanwhile, Criteo reported Q3 revenue of $416 million, down 11%, while contribution ex-TAC, i.e., after “traffic acquisition costs”, declined 17% to $240 million.

Criteo’s retail media revenue fell 23%, partly reflecting changes involving two (unnamed) clients that reduced business by $20 million, according to executives there.

Meanwhile, Taboola reported revenue of $462.3 million, down 6.9%, amid declining advertising impression volumes. However, the company’s gross profit rose 9.6% to $152.3 million despite the 6.9% revenue decline.

By contrast, Teads (Taboola’s historic rival and one-time prospective merger partner) recorded the largest decline, with revenue falling 26% to $235.1 million. After TAC, its gross profit declined 28% to $93.5 million, according to the filing, with execs there citing continued weakness in open-internet advertising and not reinstating its financial guidance. In total, this equated to a net loss of $325.5 million, compared with a $19.7 million loss a year earlier.

Revenue growth did not consistently align with profitability changes across the six companies in this cohort, with Unity reporting its first profitable quarter since its IPO (see chart below).

By contrast, Teads (Taboola’s historic rival, and one-time prospective merger partner) recorded the largest decline, with revenue falling 26% to $235.1 million. After TAC, its gross profit declined 28% to $93.5 million, according to the filing, with execs there citing continued weakness in open-internet advertising and not reinstating its financial guidance. In total, this equated to a net loss of $325.5 million, compared with a $19.7 million loss a year earlier.

Revenue growth did not consistently correspond with changes in profitability across the six companies in this cohort, with Unity’s fortunes contrasting to Criteo – the company that was initially renowned as an ad-retargeting company, but now preferring to underline its retail media wares – reporting a deterioration in both revenue and profitability. Criteo’s gross margin declined from 55% to 50%, while net income fell 85% to $6 million. The figures show that companies reporting similar revenue trends are experiencing different outcomes in terms of gross profit, net income and cash generation.

For all the (inevitable) talk of AI, CTV remained a significant source of growth for Magnite and Teads during the quarter.

Magnite reported a 39% increase in CTV contribution, ex-TAC, to $105.4 million, compared with 5% growth in its DV+ business, which includes display and other digital video advertising.

CTV accounted for approximately 53% of Magnite’s total contribution, ex-TAC, of $200.6 million, compared with 45% a year earlier. Overall revenue increased 14% to $204.2 million, while total contribution ex-TAC grew 20%.

CTV growth also proved an oasis for Outbrain’s executive team, with revenue from the channel increasing 37% year over year. However, this was offset by the fact that CTV represented just 12% of the company’s quarterly revenue, compared with its overall revenue decline of 26%. Teads reported nearly $100 million in year-to-date CTV revenue.

Taken in tandem, these results show that CTV continues to grow despite weaker performance in other areas of digital advertising. However, the contribution of that growth to overall company performance depends on the CTV business’s size relative to other revenue streams.

The filings mark the midway point in this quarter’s earnings season, with Zeta Global, The Trade Desk, PubMatic and Lifoff Mobile all due to make their equivalent filings next week.

Instagram wants to pull long-form creators away from YouTube, but is it offering enough? Experts weigh in on the platform’s pivot.

DoorDash’s announced plans to acquire Instacart for $17 billion point to the constant need to gain market share by diversifying services and offerings.

Creators see promise in Instagram’s long-form push, but question whether exposure is enough

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Join us Dec. 1-3 in Nashville for the Digiday Programmatic Marketing Summit

Last week, Instagram said users would be able to post videos up to 90 minutes long, a move aimed at attracting long-form YouTube creators. But, aside from offering a handful of select creators between $50,000 and $500,000 to post long-form content through its new “Features” feature, it has offered no concrete monetization pathway for creators, saying it’s “too early to share a long-form monetization model.”

Early reactions are mixed: Without a clear ad revenue plan, creators may hesitate to make original content for Instagram, while others see promise in the platform’s discovery tools and new TV app.

Digiday spoke with seven creator economy experts about what the change could mean for creators, agents, and marketers.

Instagram is known for reach, but engagement on Reels can vary (marketing software platform Cropink reports Reels get 36% more reach than carousels and 125% more than single-photo posts, but engagement lags). And creators question how that engagement translates into income.

Digital strategist and creator Gabriella Zutrau said that Instagram has great discoverability compared to other platforms, which is helpful for creators looking to build awareness. However, she was “disgusted” that it appears, at least for now, that the platform is “offering discoverability as a form of payment.”

Several of the creator managers and creators Digiday spoke with expressed concerns about the lack of an ad revenue structure for long-form content — especially in comparison to what is widely considered the best platform for earning, YouTube. But it’s increasingly difficult to get monetized on YouTube because of the thresholds required, and it may be harder after its requirements change next year.

“Exposure as a form of payment and with the intention of cutting into YouTube’s market share and cutting into the amount of views people are getting on YouTube — it’s just gross,” Zutrau said. “Instagram has never valued creators.”

Jo Wong, chief revenue officer at agentic AI creator commerce platform POP.STORE, agreed.

“If you talk to most creators, they’ll tell you that that’s really where a lot of the brand deals come through… Instagram in general never really had a super strong monetization program for creators off the get,” said Wong. “Frankly, I think they thought they could get away with [not having a long-form monetization model].”

Samantha Walters, vp of talent experience at Shine Talent Group, said she was “surprised” the platform hadn’t offered a competitive or comparable monetization infrastructure. “That’s why YouTube is so successful, and why creators give so much of their time and effort to it,” she added.

Nick Jacklin, president of Shorthand Studios, was more optimistic, though he emphasized that creators should be paid equitably and transparently, and noted that if Instagram starts running ads on long-form content, it’ll break creator trust.

“I’m all in favor of advocating for a similar model [to YouTube] where Instagram makes $1 [per view], some share of that goes to the creator…but if Instagram can show with the creators that do [Features] work early on that you can drive awareness, I think the monetization will follow,” Jacklin said.

A representative from Instagram told Digiday the platform is “exploring ways for creators to earn from brands, fans and Instagram, including performance-based incentives.”

For Alexandra Mathieu, vp of creative and strategy at Open Influence, the competition Instagram’s long-form push could breed is good for talent. “Instagram putting money behind creator acquisition gives managers more leverage in conversations with YouTube,” she said. “It also opens the door to negotiating beyond fees, including exclusivity, distribution, production support, and creative control.”

With monetization up in the air, are creators and creator agencies planning to do a strategic pivot? It depends on the content genre, it seems.

Mustafa Aijaz, vp of media company SoaR, which works with a lot of gamers and streamers, plans to use it as just another distribution platform for now. “We’ll post our content there, why not? But I’m not making content for Instagram at this moment in time,” he said.

Zack Evans, vp of creative strategy at Shorthand Studios, thinks interview-style shows and scripted comedy could work really well on the platform — especially with the revamped Instagram TV app, which he said promotes scrolling and discoverability more than YouTube does.

“Comedy is inherently surprising, so I think with Reels and Features now, being able to just scroll and see a scripted thing playing out, you don’t yet know what it is, and you’re going to be intrigued,” he said. “It’s much more a pure comedy experience…I think that there will be a certain sector of creators who will actually rise to [this] occasion.”

The ability to pull viewers in with short-form content and funnel them to long-form while remaining on the platform could be a boon for any kind of video creator.

“The top of the funnel is Instagram. [We’re always] trying to get them and trying to use Reels, use Shorts, use any kind of way to grab attention for people, and then try to funnel them over to a longer-form piece of content,” said Evans. “It’s really hard, and these platforms obviously aren’t the best of friends, and so they make it difficult for you to do that.”

The discoverability of short-form and the shareability of content on Instagram could help creators build bigger audiences on the platform, which they can then shop to brands.

Both Wong and Mathieu suggested creators might use Instagram’s Features to recycle older content for reach.

“I’ve encouraged creators to actually use their older content and put it on there,” said Wong. “Most of the views on your YouTube come within the first month, for example, or three weeks, then put your older catalog on Features and test into it. See how your reach is doing. What does it mean for you? Are you able to then get better brand deals, or how does that change?”

She’s advising creators to do that before “catalyzing” original content, whereas Mathieu thinks creators should use Features to use “existing programming as a springboard for new, Instagram-native series such as creator-led shows, movie-length content, longer-running series and video.”

It’s clear that creators are still sussing out Instagram’s latest feature. In a survey sent to 50 of Shine Talent Group’s creators, 93 percent said the platform would have to change its revenue policies to entice them to post long-form content; 100 percent already post on the platform.

Ad tech’s winners and losers emerge as CTV growth masks open-web struggles.

49 Years Later, Steven Spielberg’s Sci-Fi Thriller Masterpiece Is Officially Free on YouTube

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Make a big batch of mashed potatoes and sit down to watch

Most creators are lucky if they ever make one truly revolutionary thing in their lives. Steven Spielberg has made at least six.

He's had his misfires and genuine failures along the way, but Spielberg has reshaped multiple genres and filmmaking as a whole in his wake. Many of his movies have become part of the bedrock of worldwide culture. There are multiple different trends and revivals that can be traced straight back to Spielberg, from Indiana Jones' faux-serial period action to Saving Private Ryan redefining war films to virtually every monster movie made after 1975.

In retrospect, however, there's one entertainment subgenre that we might not give Spielberg enough credit for, and that's the alien conspiracy thriller. 1977's Close Encounters of the Third Kind was Spielberg's second big hit after Jaws. While it hasn't latched onto the popular imagination in quite the way that some of Spielberg's later films did, it's hard not to see Close Encounters' influence on the subsequent alien-abduction films and TV of the 1980s and 1990s.

If you're interested in seeing Close Encounters for yourself in advance of its 50th anniversary, YouTube is currently airing it for free, albeit with ads. Even better, YouTube's version is the Spielberg-endorsed Director's Cut from 1998, which reverses some changes that were made to the film in its 1980 Special Edition.

When I was a kid, I always thought Close Encounters was the third film in a series, based solely upon the title. In fact, the name comes from the 1972 book The UFO Experience: A Scientific Inquiry, by J. Allen Hynek. Hynek was a scientific consultant on Close Encounters, and even makes a cameo at one point in the film. (Look for the guy at Devils Tower smoking the pipe.)

In his book, Hynek proposed a scale of classification for UFO sightings, which ranged from #1 (strange lights in the sky) to #6 (encountering a UFO that comes alongside an 'animated entity,' such as a human pilot or genuine alien). The latter three stages on Hynek's scale are labeled as "close encounters," the last and most significant of which, the “third kind,” provided the final name for the film. It’s another term for what’s now more commonly called “first contact.”

Close Encounters opens in 1977, as multiple lost aircraft have suddenly reappeared in the middle of the California desert. As a team of scientists tries to figure out what's happened, multiple UFOs are sighted all over the world.

In Muncie, Indiana, Roy Neary (Richard Dreyfuss) is called out to address a local power outage. While working, a near-miss from a passing UFO burns half of Roy's face.

After that encounter, Roy quickly becomes obsessed with the UFOs, which endangers his relationship with his wife Ronnie (Teri Garr) and their children. Without any idea why, Roy is increasingly compelled to make models and sculptures of a specific mountain with whatever's handy, from dirt to clay to mashed potatoes. What Roy doesn't know is that his new hobby is about to intersect with what's become a genuine conspiracy.

There are parts of Close Encounters that can be a genuinely rough sit. While the audience knows that Roy is on to something, due to how his obsessions intersect with other characters and scenes, Roy himself doesn't. The second act of the movie is a portrait of psychological disintegration, as Roy himself doesn't know what's happened to him. He ends up alone in his house at one point, surrounded by news clippings and handmade models, in a scene that presages all the great conspiracy-board scenes in later pop culture, from The X-Files to that famous meme of Charlie Day from It's Always Sunny in Philadelphia. In retrospect, Roy Neary is a prototype for what’s since become a famous stock character in Western fiction: the conspiracy-monger who’s just looking for that one last “I told you so!” that will prove them right.

That might be why Close Encounters has faded slightly in the popular imagination. It's a science fiction movie, but it doesn't have the spectacle or excitement of Spielberg's later work in the genre. In fact, he revisited the topic of alien conspiracies himself five years later with E.T., which, in retrospect, might've overshadowed what hype Close Encounters may have had. Richard Dreyfuss going slowly insane at the family dinner table isn't quite as iconic an image as E.T.'s bicycle chase.

Just the same, Close Encounters is a classic for a reason, and almost 50 years later, you can make a real case that the real conspiracy is how a movie this good ever wound up becoming so overlooked. As long as you know going in that it's closer to a strange family drama than any kind of thriller, it's worth seeing Close Encounters of the Third Kind at least once. It's an underrated linchpin of the last 50 years in science fiction.

Close Encounters of the Third Kind’s Director's Cut is currently streaming on YouTube.

Star Trek: Child Of The Collective Officially Brings Back Picard's Son In Full Borg Form

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Jack Crusher's introduction in Star Trek: Picard brought one of the biggest revelations in the show's history, confirming that Jean-Luc Picard and Beverly Crusher had secretly welcomed a son together. However, Jack's mysterious connection to his father's past would ultimately lead to one of the darkest and most twisted storylines in the franchise.

Throughout Picard's third and final season, Jack experienced terrifying visions and discovered unusual abilities that he struggled to explain. Eventually, he learned that he'd inherited a genetic connection to the Borg through his father's previous assimilation as Locutus. This made him extremely valuable to the Borg Queen, who goes on to assimilate Jack and transform him into Võx, Child of the Collective.

Fortunately, Picard managed to rescue his son during the series finale, freeing him from the Borg's control and allowing Jack to reclaim his humanity. Now, Star Trek is bringing back Jack Crusher in full Borg form in the upcoming release of "Vox, Child of the Collective" as part of a major new crossover.

Jack Crusher's Borg transformation is officially returning in Magic: The Gathering's upcoming Star Trek crossover through "Võx, Child of the Collective," a new card featured in the We Are the Borg Commander deck. The card depicts Picard's son in his fully assimilated form, revisiting his brief but terrifying transformation during Star Trek: Picard Season 3.

The card is classified as a rare Legendary Artifact Creature—Borg, with a power and toughness of 3/3 and a mana cost of five, including one blue mana. Its special ability activates when Võx enters the battlefield and at the beginning of the player's first main phase, granting the next artifact creature and Spacecraft spells cast that turn the undaunted ability. This will reduce their mana costs based on the number of opponents, potentially making it much easier to play powerful Borg units and ships.

Jack Borg form in We Are the Borg is truly fitting considering the deck's theme around assimilation and expanding the Collective. Led by the Borg Queen, the deck lets players strengthen their artifact creatures, assimilate opposing forces, build a dangerous Borg army, etc. It also features Locutus of Borg, meaning players can reunite Jack with his father's assimilated form too, alongside other recognizable characters like Seven of Nine and Hugh.

We Are the Borg is one of four Commander decks arriving with the crossover, alongside Federation Fleet, led by Jean-Luc Picard; Landing Party, featuring Spock; and Klingon Fury, led by Worf. Each includes 100 cards, with 32 new-to-Magic cards.

However, Wizards of the Coast has confirmed production and shipping delays affecting Commander decks in North America, warning that some products may arrive after the planned November 13 release. Certain products in other regions are also affected, although no revised release date has been announced.

Fortunately, the larger Magic: The Gathering x Star Trek crossover is still scheduled for November 13, 2026, with pre-release events beginning today, November 6, and a digital launch on MTG Arena on November 10. Celebrating 60 years of Star Trek, the ambitious crossover brings together iconic characters, starships, enemies, technology, and dozens of iconic moments from across the franchise in one massive collection.

Oracle vs. Alphabet: Which Is the Better AI Cloud Stock to Own for the Next 5 Years?

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Both companies are in the middle of an AI spending spree, but Alphabet's prominent position in cloud computing gives it the edge.

There's a growing list of technology companies investing heavily in artificial intelligence (AI) cloud computing as demand for compute capacity accelerates. Bank of America estimates that data center infrastructure spending will more than double to $2.1 trillion by 2028.

But not every company that's spending money now on AI cloud computing will have substantial sales and earnings to show for it later.

Alphabet (GOOGL -2.20%) (GOOG -1.93%) and Oracle (ORCL -6.93%) are two prominent cloud companies ramping up their AI investments right now. Still, while Alphabet's cloud sales are accelerating due to its spending, Oracle's debt is worrying shareholders.

Investors wondering which is the better AI cloud stock to own over the next five years need only look at Google Cloud's third-quarter sales doubling for their answer. Here's why Alphabet is the safer long-term bet.

Alphabet stock fell hard recently amid investor concerns about its high capital expenditures, which are expected to reach up to $205 billion this year. The spending has resulted in $9.1 billion in negative free cash flow, worrying many Alphabet shareholders.

But investors seemed to misunderstand two factors: First, $5.1 billion of the negative free cash flow was from a one-time fee to the European Commission, making the decline not as bad as it first appears. The second is that Alphabet's AI spending already appears to be paying off amid rising Google Cloud sales.

Consider that Google Cloud sales more than doubled in the third quarter (which ended Sept. 30) to $30.7 billion. That's an exceptionally high growth rate and shows why the company's capex is rising so quickly.

Rising demand for computing capacity is encouraging Alphabet to ramp up spending, and as expensive as it is, demand appears to continue outpacing capacity. The company said Google Cloud has a $561 billion cloud backlog, which it estimates will convert to sales over the next 24 months.

Negative free cash flow isn't great to see, I'll admit. But the company can always pull back spending once it has enough compute capacity. And when it does, its soaring cloud sales will likely help bring the company's free cash flow back into positive territory -- especially considering that Google Cloud's operating income recently tripled to $11 billion and operating margins have increased to 36%.

Oracle is also investing heavily in data center infrastructure, with management saying that capital expenditures will total up to $95 billion this year. That's up from $55.7 billion in fiscal 2026.

Investors have been skeptical about Oracle's accelerating spending, and they really haven't liked how the company is paying for it by taking on debt. To ease concerns, leadership recently said it would limit its capital raises through fiscal 2030 to an additional $20 billion (on top of an additional $20 billion raised recently).

While that temporarily eased investor fears, they came roaring back just days later when Oracle announced it would raise all of that $20 billion immediately rather than over the next few years. Oracle has about $169 billion in total debt, which has spooked investors and resulted in Oracle's stock falling 50% over the past year.

It may not be all bad news for Oracle investors, though, as management says free cash flow could be $30 billion or more in fiscal 2029 -- a stark contrast to its negative free cash flow of $23.7 billion in fiscal 2026.

If you feel comfortable with some additional risk, Oracle stock could be a good investment as the company's spending slows and free cash flow rebounds over the next couple of years.

But I think Alphabet looks like the better AI cloud stock over the next five years. It's already the third-largest cloud player and is poised to generate significant sales from its massive Google Cloud backlog.

Bank of America is an advertising partner of Motley Fool Money. Chris Neiger has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Alphabet and Oracle. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

4Space draws on cultural significance of Arabian horses for UAE restaurant

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Design studio 4Space “explored the physical and kinetic qualities” of horses for the fluid, organic design of Falwa, an Abu Dhabi restaurant celebrating modern Emirati cuisine.

Located in the Abu Dhabi Royal Equestrian Art (ADREA) academy, the contemporary restaurant is led by Michelin-starred Emirati chef Faisal Alharmoodi.

To showcase the importance of the Arabian mare as a cultural icon in this equestrian space, 4Space designed the restaurant to represent abstract depictions of horses and their anatomy.

“The design team explored the physical and kinetic qualities of the animal itself, specifically its skeletal structure, muscular tension, and fluid movement,” 4Space said.

It aimed to convey the animal’s cultural and historical importance through architecture, using an organic design language throughout the restaurant, which fittingly opened in 2026 – the Year of the Horse.

Named Falwa after the Arabic term for a young female mare in her first year of life, the eatery has a fluid interior with stretched, curved walls designed to evoke horses in motion.

The restaurant interior also references a specific horse named Rabdan, the late Sheikh Zayed bin Sultan Al Nahyan’s, which inspired the custom-made dusty white mineral plaster that wraps its walls and adds a tactile feel.

“This earthier, softer alternative to pure white acts as a passive canvas for the shifting daylight, allowing natural and artificial light to paint dynamic shadows across the curved walls, evoking the powerful presence and coat of the horse,” 4Space explained.

Rabdan’s coat also influenced Falwa’s overall colour palette, which features warm reddish, tan and brown hues against the white walls.

“By utilising rich crimson tones and warm, tactile materials, the design team evokes the powerful presence and coat of this legendary steed,” 4Space explained.

Falwa’s tactile, organic feel of the walls is contrasted by the polished, glossy flooring, which was made from Breccia Aurora marble. Its beige and brown veining matches the restaurant’s earthy brown leather and wood tones.

While the restaurant mostly features abstract references to Arabian horses, 4Space also added more specific equestrian details to the interior.

4Space created custom-made dining chairs with decorative leather straps, visible stitching and metal hardware in a nod to horse saddles and tack.

Above the central tables at the restaurant, a tactile sculpture with a skeletal, undulating design once again evokes movement and anatomy.

4Space also used the Vibia Plusminus lighting system by designer Stefan Diez for Falwa. Its signature textile straps, from which glass globes are suspended, match the equestrian leather straps on the chairs and add a playful effect to the interior.

Overall, 4Space aimed to tie Falwa’s food and interior together, with both aspects of the restaurant functioning as contemporary expressions of Emirati culture and heritage.

“The design demonstrates how a deep cultural relationship can be expressed as a living, modern architectural language,” the studio concluded.

To learn more about 4Space, visit its website.

Moon phase today: What the Moon will look like on November 6, 2026

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

You'll need a good pair of binoculars or a telescope to get a closer look at what's on the Moon's surface tonight, and one of the last chances before the New Moon arrives.

As of Friday, Nov. 6, NASA's Daily Moon Guide tracker tells us the Moon is in the Waning Crescent phase, with 16% of its surface visible.

To see the Grimaldi Basin tonight, you'll need the assistance of either binoculars or a telescope.

The next Full Moon will take place on November 24.

It takes the Moon around 29.5 days to pass through a complete lunar cycle. As it travels around Earth, the angle at which sunlight reaches its surface changes what we can see from here, producing the different phases, from the New Moon through to the Full Moon and back again. The cycle then starts over, as explained by NASA.

New Moon - The Moon is between Earth and the sun, so the side we see is dark (in other words, it's invisible to the eye).

Waxing Crescent - A small sliver of light appears on the right side (Northern Hemisphere).

First Quarter - Half of the Moon is lit on the right side. It looks like a half-Moon.

Waxing Gibbous - More than half is lit up, but it's not quite full yet.

Full Moon - The whole face of the Moon is illuminated and fully visible.

Waning Gibbous - The Moon starts losing light on the right side. (Northern Hemisphere)

Third Quarter (or Last Quarter) - Another half-Moon, but now the left side is lit.

Waning Crescent - A thin sliver of light remains on the left side before going dark again.

Lois Mackenzie is a freelance reporter at Mashable. Over the years she has written for many publications, covering everything from the local news to the best pair of running shoes. You can find bylines in publications including Fit&Well, Metro, and Coach magazine, usually covering deals on everything from earbuds to TVs, or guides on how to beat your half marathon time.

Lois also holds a Master's degree in Digital Journalism from Strathclyde University and obtained a Master of Arts in English Literature at the University of Aberdeen.

Hurdle hints and answers for November 6, 2026

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

If you like playing daily word games like Wordle, then Hurdle is a great game to add to your routine.

There are five rounds to the game. The first round sees you trying to guess the word, with correct, misplaced, and incorrect letters shown in each guess. If you guess the correct answer, it'll take you to the next hurdle, providing the answer to the last hurdle as your first guess. This can give you several clues or none, depending on the words. For the final hurdle, every correct answer from previous hurdles is shown, with correct and misplaced letters clearly shown.

An important note is that the number of times a letter is highlighted from previous guesses does necessarily indicate the number of times that letter appears in the final hurdle.

How Can Europe Accelerate Electric Trucking?

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Europe is having a hard time lately, with skyrocketing oil prices dampening economies. Fuel costs, particularly diesel, are prompting surging interest in truck electrification. Europe has been urging truck makers to move toward electric trucking, and they have responded with a variety of electric semi trucks from many manufacturers. Europe is a group of sovereign countries with many standards. The European Union is a political and economic union of 27 states in that area. It issued directives urging action and imposing penalties. In response to European Union directives, companies have formed EV100, a coalition of corporations responding to the need. In a recent story, Steve Hanley wrote about European electric truck makers’ difficulties expanding truck electrification. Here’s a quotation from the story: “In a letter to EU Commission president Ursula von der Leyen, of primary concern, in their estimation, is expanding the charging infrastructure for heavy-duty trucks in Europe.” There is no shortage of electric trucks in Europe. Expansion could go faster were it not for charging infrastructure. Manufacturers complain about “an alarming delay” in conditions needed to drive market uptake. For Europe, truck electrification is not working as well as in China, where over 90% of the world’s electric trucks reside. The differences between China and the EU show some reasons why truck electrification is slower in Europe. How did China succeed where the EU failed? China made electric trucks a freight system, not a vehicle category.

Power is a major factor in electric grid infrastructure. Charging electric trucks places demands on power. Peak power determines how much infrastructure is necessary. In grid planning, peak annual power determines how much capital equipment is necessary. Reducing peak power demand is a critical factor for electric truck infrastructure. Figure 1 shows one method of reducing power called route stuffing. It schedules departures on heavy traffic corridors at regularly spaced intervals. Corridors like ones between ports and warehouses are a target for those practices. These corridors often route trucks in a round trip chain. Filling routes with scheduled trips can reduce power demand compared to random trip scheduling. The article Battery Swap Uses Fewer Batteries Than You Think is devoted to methods of analysis and planning that can lead to lower peak power demand. There are other benefits to planning, such as greater truck load capacity from the same amount of batteries. Part of the solution to slow electric truck infrastructure development is to plan infrastructure and truck use practices to reduce the charging power burden. Given the pressing and enduring need due to the diesel fuel cost crisis, it is necessary to reprioritize grid power upgrade demands. Demands for data center power upgrades like transformers for substations should be re shuffled and prioritized toward the diesel crisis.

In two articles, Michael Barnard outlined the issues. Vehicles are not a transportation system. What does Europe have now? It has an EU Commission interacting with a coalition, EV100. The problem is, there are plenty of trucks available, but who is in charge of infrastructure? You can’t have electric trucks without means of charging, but as the coalition complains, “Significant improvements to charging infrastructure, a reduction in carbon dioxide based tolls, and a coherent policy framework are urgently needed.” There are so many stakeholders — including the commission, vehicles makers, and charging companies across multiple countries — that no one is in charge of infrastructure, and without it, nothing moves. It needs a unity of purpose and action.

If China is succeeding, why not adopt its strategy? Perhaps the answer is right in front of everyone’s nose. As Michael puts it, “the policy connects vehicles to corridors, depots, service areas, grids and freight use cases instead of treating sales targets as sufficient.” CATL offered Europe a system, not a vehicle. This is a “turn key” solution. It’s a well thought out system, including use of methods and practices, not an anything goes free-for-all. That kind of stability and predictability is what EV100 leaders are looking for. This is not a swap-or-nothing scenario. Different techniques apply to various use cases. Swap must be planned for high-use freight corridors and other proper applications. Swap trucks can also fast charge or slow charge, providing flexibility and a system of trucks that includes smaller trucks, not just semis.

What does all this have to do with Europe’s slowly advancing infrastructure? Simply put, electric trucks put heavy charging demands on the grid. Trucks can have megawatt-hour batteries that require megawatt power charging to charge in an hour. Reducing grid power demands can reduce grid infrastructure upgrade costs, and leverage capital investment toward faster implementation.

“The process of getting megawatt charging equipment connected to the grid can take several years, delaying deployment even where operators and infrastructure providers are ready to invest.” The need for megawatt charging equipment is reduced by accelerating swap network development instead of fixed battery trucks that must be charged by fast chargers. “Swaptopus is easy to misread as a quirky name for another electric-truck infrastructure project. It is a newly formed joint venture between Octopus Energy and CATL, created to develop automated battery-swapping hubs for heavy trucks across Europe.” Unlike the government of China that played a role in encouraging standards and cooperation in infrastructure, Europe has a looser arrangement between many countries with various standards. Europe is learning the slow, hard way where battery swapping is most needed.

Every truck application has different optimization. It means that there must be different approaches to fit freight operations in heavy traffic corridors. Quoting Michael Barnard, “Freight operators often run repeated routes between ports, factories, distribution centres and logistics hubs. Those patterns provide known traffic, known stopping points and relatively predictable electricity demand. A swap hub can be placed around an existing freight flow instead of hoping that enough unrelated trucks eventually pass through.”

Freight corridors are a scenario made to order for battery swap. Real trucking solutions include a mix of vehicle and infrastructure types — from small, local delivery, to large, long-haul freight; and from slow, overnight charging, to medium power charging and battery swap — all used in concert to accelerate heavy truck electrification. With a wide variety of charging options, and vehicles and charging use cases targeted in coordination to avoid peak power demands, the path forward is eased. School buses don’t need expensive high-power fast charging. Lower-power chargers can do the trick. Local delivery can use a mix of overnight and medium power charging. Heavy truck dispatch must be more carefully coordinated to avoid peak demand and grid upgrade costs. A mix of medium-power charging and swap stations along with planned use cases can successfully lower electric freight power needs and transport system capital expenses to speed transport electrification.

Transformation and conversion of diesel freight systems to electric requires coordination and planning between government agencies and coalitions of corporations, cities, government agencies, and planners. Transportation is a system basic to societies and economics. Coordination between groups at all levels results in practices, use cases, and infrastructure that produce benefits for all. Grid expansion and construction can take years. In order for Europe to implement truck freight electrification, more structure and planning is needed to minimize grid power requirements, speed implementation, and maximize capital investment results. Transportation system, practices, and infrastructure must be planned for optimum results.

has studied wind, electric vehicles, and environmental issues. An electrical engineer familiar with power and electronics, he has participated in the Automotive X Prize contest. He is an avid writer, specializing in electric vehicles, batteries, and wind energy.

‘It Would Take a Lot to Say No to Australia’: Foo Fighters Kick-Off Tour Down Under With Three-Hours of Power

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

The Foos were back on familiar turf for their Take Cover Australian tour opener at Suncorp Stadium in Brisbane, the noisy neighbor to the XXXX brewery.

Australia is at the mercy of factors beyond anyone’s control. Extreme weather, the tyranny of distance. This great southern land is at the ass-end of the world, a journey that that will take many travelers a day out of their life to get there, and another to return home.

In rocks the Foo Fighters, again, urging you to hold their beer. In January of this year, the U.S. band completed the long-haul for a one-off show in Launceston, the second city of Tasmania, an island some 1,600 miles north of Antarctica. On that occasion, the Foos spent 48 hours traveling, for a three-hour show at UTAS Stadium. Mission accomplished. Four years earlier, in 2022, Dave Grohl and Co. performed for one-night only at GMHBA Stadium in Geelong, a satellite city of Melbourne, to help refuel a touring industry crippled by the lockdown.

On Thursday night (Nov. 5), the Foos were back on familiar turf for their Take Cover Australian tour opener at Suncorp Stadium in Brisbane, the noisy neighbor to the XXXX brewery. It’s their second visit to this country in 10 months. In a quirk of the schedule, the band will break after the Adelaide date on the tour, Nov. 17, and return in late January for New Zealand shows and a tour closer in Perth. Count them. That’s three visits to Australia in a year, all of them for concerts produced by Frontier Touring.

The Foos love Australia, and it’s reciprocated. Guitarist Chris Shiflett stepped out wearing an AC/DC shirt, with the sleeves hacked off. Grohl recounted his three-decade-long affair with the country, before the band played “Big Me” from their self-titled 1995 debut album. The parody “Footos” music video, Grohl revealed, was shot here. The Foos might as well have Aussie passports at this point.

“I’m so f—ing grateful, and lucky, that we’re still here 30 years later, and coming to tour for you guys again,” Grohl told his audience. “It would take a lot to say no to Australia.”

From the gritty staccato chords of “All My Life” and Grohl’s “are you f—ing ready” pep talk, through to the Foos’ traditional ending number “Everlong,” Billboard soaked up the action on a glorious spring night.

Check out four outstanding moments below.

There’s no mystery to a Foo Fighters show. You know what’s coming. Stadium rock, old favorites, hits, energy. It’s a meal that’s both nutritious and heavy enough that you don’t leave hungry.

The Rock Hall-inducted band paid it back to Generation X, the “old school,” many of whom were in the crowd, with their kids along for the ride. The first 10 songs was a greatest hits set, opening, as the Foos do, with “All My Life.” One by one they knocked them down. “The Pretender, “Times Like These,” “Rope,” “Stacked Actors,” My Hero,” “Learning to Fly.” These guys don’t come down to land.

Starting at 7.30pm, early even for this city whose residents have the reputation as the earliest risers on earth, the band blasted through 26 songs, finishing at 10.30pm. Three solid hours of power.

Grohl doesn’t just sing, scream and play the guitar (sometimes he drums, too). The rocker isn’t shy of saying some words, either, with the tens of thousands gathered before him.

“Welcome to the first night of our Australian tour,” he said over the arpeggios of “These Days.” “First time we came here, I think it was 30 years ago…the first time I went to Australia. It was a tour called the Summersault tour. Actually, there’s a movie about it. It’s called The Best Summer, you should watch that. Because we were little f—ing kids, man. That was a long time ago. I look back on that now. And I think… I’m so fucking grateful, and lucky, that we’re still here 30 years later, and coming to tour for you guys again. It would take a lot to say no to Australia.”

And “sometimes we only come out for 24 f—ing hours to play a show,” a reference to those Geelong and Launceston dates. Tonight, he promised, “we’re going to play as many songs as we can. It’s why we play song after song, after song after song. We got so many f—ing songs. But we’ll try to play as long as we can for you. We got some of that old school motherf—ers.” Later, Grohl explained that he faceplanted on the Suncorp Stadium stage, or “ate sh–” in his words. Still, for a guy who broke a leg after falling during a performance in Sweden back in 2015, it was the type of injury he could walk off.

Taylor Hawkins is no longer with us, but not forgotten. The band paid tribute to the beloved drummer with a performance of 1999’s “Aurora.” “This song is very special to us,” Grohl said. “We play it every night for Taylor Hawkins.”

With all those “old school” songs, and newbies to select from the band’s 12th and latest album Your Favorite Toy, the Foos still found time to play some covers. The band burned rubber with a cover of “Ace of Spades” by Motorhead, the British heavy metal legends that supported the Foos in the past and whose singer, the late Lemmy Kilmister, was a good friend of Grohl. And, with the penultimate song of the show, performed Gerry Rafferty’s “Baker Street,” with Shiflett reimagining that famous sax riff with his axe.

The Foos also crunched “Marigold,” which Grohl first released in 1992 on his solo cassette project Pocketwatch, and later re-recorded with Nirvana as the b-side to 1993’s “Heart-Shaped Box.” “We don’t do it often, and haven’t done in a long time,” he explained, as he dedicated the song “to all the Nirvana people here tonight.” Judging by the volume of Nirvana t-shirts in the audience, there were many.

Australians got, for the first time, a close look at the six-strong Foos lineup featuring Grohl, Shiflett, Nate Mendel (bass), Pat Smear (guitar), new drummer Ilan Rubin, and keyboardist Rami Jaffee. Rubin, playing behind one of the better drummers in the business, and succeeding the master Hawkins, had big shoes to fill. He didn’t miss a beat, and left no doubt about his supreme talents with several drum solos.

In a fun spot, each of Grohl’s bandmates had a crack at playing a song from an earlier band. When introducing Nate’s song “Seven,” from his previous life with Seattle’s Sunny Day Real Estate, Grohl ripped out an audible burp. With live music, always expect the unexpected.

And when it came time for Rubin for play “Tap Dancing in a Minefield,” he did a switcheroo with Grohl which put the two-time Rock And Roll Hall of Fame inductee back behind the kit, for a few minutes at least.

Done, and onto the next one this Saturday, Nov. 7 at Queensland Country Bank Stadium in Townsville, north Queensland.

Chipotle Mexican Grill vs. e.l.f. Beauty: Which Consumer Stock Is a Better Buy?

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Comparing Chipotle to e.l.f. reveals vastly different risk-reward profiles.

Investors choosing between Chipotle Mexican Grill (CMG +0.57%) and e.l.f. Beauty (ELF -3.33%) face a classic choice between steady restaurant growth and a high-momentum beauty disruptor.

Chipotle relies on its reputation for fresh ingredients and digital convenience to drive volume, while e.l.f. Beauty uses a multi-brand strategy and mass-market retail partnerships to reach younger, value-conscious consumers. Both companies are scaling rapidly while navigating different economic pressures.

Chipotle operates a massive network of company-owned restaurants focused on high-quality ingredients and efficient service across several countries. The company has successfully leaned into digital sales, which accounted for nearly 36.7% of food and beverage revenue in its latest annual report. While expanding its physical footprint, it also relies on third-party delivery services, showing a strong presence among retail stocks in the dining category.

In the fiscal year ended Dec. 31, 2025, revenue reached nearly $11.9 billion, representing growth of roughly 5.4% compared with the prior fiscal year. The net margin, which is the percentage of revenue remaining as profit after all costs, was approximately 12.9%. This resulted in a net income of close to $1.5 billion, demonstrating the company's ability to maintain consistent profitability despite rising commodity prices.

e.l.f. Beauty provides cosmetics through an omni-channel strategy that blends e-commerce with major retail partnerships. The company maintains a small number of critical relationships with Target (TGT -3.16%), Walmart (WMT -1.12%), Amazon (AMZN -2.19%), and Sephora, which together represent a significant portion of its sales. Its multi-brand approach, including the recently acquired rhode, allows it to target more of the beauty market.

In the fiscal year ended March 31, 2026, revenue reached nearly $1.6 billion, a significant increase of approximately 24.6% over the previous year. However, the net margin was roughly 1.6%, leading to a net income of about $26.3 million for the period. This lower profitability relative to previous years reflects the costs associated with integrating new brands and expanding its global footprint.

Chipotle Mexican Grill faces significant risks regarding food safety, as any incident of food-borne illness could severely damage its brand reputation. The company operates in a highly competitive industry and must constantly balance price and quality to retain customers. Furthermore, it deals with supply chain dependencies and complex labor regulations, including potential unionization efforts and scheduling laws that could increase operating costs.

e.l.f. Beauty faces intense competition from established giants like Coty (COTY +12.68%) and L'Oréal (LRLCF +1.40%), which could lead to a loss of valuable retail shelf space. The company relies heavily on a supply chain based in China, making it vulnerable to trade policies, tariffs, and logistics disruptions. Integration risks also persist with its acquisition of rhode, which depends heavily on the continued involvement and public image of key personnel.

Chipotle carries a lower earnings multiple but a similar sales-based valuation compared to the faster-growing beauty brand.

Inflation is still stubborn, interest rate hikes are on the table for the foreseeable future, fears over an artificial intelligence bubble are growing, and there are plenty of other potential unknowns that could affect the markets in 2027. That's why, between the two companies, I would rather own the one generating more net income, as that doesn't fully shield a company from downturns but can offer a safety net during volatile times. With $1.5 billion in net income in 2025 compared to e.l.f.'s $26.3 million for fiscal year 2026, Chipotle easily wins that battle.

That said, beyond its ability to generate profits, I also like that it has revenue growth opportunities. Chipotle opened its 4,000th store in 2025, with a long-term goal of operating 7,000 locations across North America. And in the third quarter of 2026 alone, it opened 84 company-owned restaurants. It's also seeing success by leaning into more digital ordering, with digital sales accounting for 38.8% of total food and beverage revenue for the third quarter. In comparison, that percentage was 36.7% in the prior-year period.

For e.l.f., there are also sales growth opportunities, as only 25% of the company's business comes from outside the United States. Still, Chipotle has a larger market it can serve, giving it more sales to convert into profits.

Jack Delaney has no position in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Amazon, Chipotle Mexican Grill, Target, and Walmart. The Motley Fool recommends e.l.f. Beauty and recommends the following options: short December 2026 $38 calls on Chipotle Mexican Grill. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.
