Fox Officially Replaces ‘9-1-1’ in 2027

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

When 9-1-1 hit the airwaves in 2018, it was a massive hit right away. With a cast boasting big names such as Angela Bassett and Peter Krause, and the pedigree of Ryan Murphy behind it, the first-responder drama instantly provided tons of fast-paced action and high-octane rescues. But by 2023, Fox was realizing that the series was costing them a pretty penny to produce. With enormous production costs of approximately $9 million an episode and a pricey licensing fee for Disney's 20th Television (which owns the rights to the show), Fox decided to throw in the towel with 9-1-1. The series moved on over to ABC, but two years later, Fox is still searching for a proper replacement for the first-responder procedural. The good news is that they just might have found it now.

In 2020, Fox released a spin-off of the original 9-1-1 series called 9-1-1: Lone Star, featuring Rob Lowe and Gina Torres at the helm and a brand-new location in Austin, Texas. However, as soon as the flagship series moved over to ABC in 2023, 9-1-1: Lone Star seemed to have lost some of its mojo (and definitely lost some of its viewers). The loss of synergy of the two shows airing on the same network definitely impacted the series, and its cancellation was announced in 2024, with its series finale airing in February 2025.

Four action series. One of them is built around exactly the way you handle pressure, people, and a room that has just gone very quiet. Here is where you would survive the trilogy, and probably headline it.

You are precision with a grudge. You don't posture, you don't escalate, and you don't warn anybody twice. That is exactly why the people who know you are so careful never to be the reason you start.

You understand the thing the whole franchise is secretly about: the cars are spectacular, but the crew is the point. You collect people, you keep them, and you'd drive through a building for any one of them.

You over-prepare and then take the dangerous part yourself anyway. It looks like control, but it's really responsibility. You have never worked out how to ask somebody else to carry the risk.

You are not the most trained person in the building, the best equipped, or the one anybody would have picked. You are simply the one still standing at the end, which turns out to be the only qualification that matters.

Fox did its best to find another first-responder procedural to air, this time locating the action in Hawaii. Rescue HI-Surf premiered in fall 2024, but was decried by critics and viewers for having way too much melodrama. Audiences just seemed to have a hard time connecting with the characters and storylines. This led to the series being cancelled after one season, leaving Fox in need of another first-responder procedural to fill the void.

The firefighters can certainly take the heat!

There have been rumblings for years now that a Baywatch reboot was being floated around Hollywood. The original series was a huge success, when it aired from 1989 to 2000. The 11-season run of the show became a pop culture obsession with those famous red bathing suits and sexy running-on-the-beach scenes. The series also demonstrated that first-responder procedurals could take place in unexpected locations with heroes that didn't look like traditional police officers or firefighters. It also helped further gender equality on television.

While some decried the women as being overly sexualized, the series also featured the female characters saving the day just as frequently as the men did. The drama also helped create mega-stars out of David Hasselhoff, Pamela Anderson, Yasmine Bleeth, and a young Jason Momoa. With the buzz the series created when it was on the air, it's not at all surprising that Fox would be interested in recreating some of that magic with its current lineup.

Fox announced that they'd be greenlighting a Baywatch revival in a straight-to-series order of 12 episodes. Several of the original creators will be returning to the project, and the showrunner will be Matt Nix, who created Burn Notice. The series is slated to premiere in January 2027, and there's already some exciting chatter about what we have in store for us. The series stars Arrow lead Stephen Amell, who plays Hobie Buchannon, the son of Mitch Buchannon (David Hasselhoff) and is a Baywatch Captain. Jessica Belkin plays Charlie Vale, Hobie's biological daughter who has just arrived in town from Texas. Hassie Harrison plays Nat, Hobie's best friend. The series also stars Noah Beck and Shay Mitchell. Original series stars David Chokachi and Erika Eleniak are back to reprise their roles, though Pamela Anderson has already confirmed she will not be joining the series. As it stands, the Baywatch procedural could be Fox's best chance at finally replacing 9-1-1.

The series already has an IP that people will recognize, which could be a reason why Rescue HI-Surf failed to take off. Most first-responder shows come with a built-in audience of some kind, and if the revival is working with some of the same team that made the original a success, Baywatch has a great chance at also making this new iteration a hit too.

Fox has been searching for the right series to finally boost their ratings back to where they were during the 9-1-1 days. Baywatch is a perfect replacement because many people will tune into the premiere just to see how close it is to the original. Any fans of the show from back in the day will likely help Fox get impressive numbers to start out with. As long as the new show brings the drama and excitement that the original was known for, the Baywatch reboot will likely stick around for some time to come.

The original Baywatch is available to watch on Tubi in the U.S.

Russell Crowe's Crime Thriller 'The Nice Guys' Resurfaces as a #1 Streaming Hit 10 Years Later

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Conor Sheeran is a Movies and TV features writer for Collider. He has a deep passion for cinema and watches everything from the biggest blockbusters to the smallest indies. While he specializes in movies and television, he has also written about politics, culture, and sports throughout his career. Based in New York City, Conor is also a massive fan of the New York Jets, Mets, and Knicks. He is still waiting for any of those teams to bring home a championship in his lifetime. A member of the National Writers Union and the International Federation of Journalists, Conor is a strong proponent of press freedom and collective action.

Sometimes, when a movie is released, audiences can almost tell immediately that they're destined to become cult classics. Whether it underperforms at the box office or with critics, it can still build an audience, simply by word of mouth, that discovers it long after it leaves theaters. The 2016 crime comedy-thriller The Nice Guys is most certainly one of these cases; despite earning critical praise, the film failed to make a financial splash and was largely forgotten. However, years after its release, audiences caught on to the movie, which is currently seeing a resurgence on streaming — in fact, The Nice Guys has soared to number one on Apple TV.

Written and directed by Shane Black, The Nice Guys transports audiences to 1970s Los Angeles, where two unlikely investigators, played by Ryan Gosling and Russell Crowe, become involved in a conspiracy surrounding a missing woman and massive corruption. What follows is a hilarious, thrilling, refreshing, and chaotic combination of detective noir, buddy comedy, and Hollywood action. Its unexpected resurgence is yet another reminder that just because a film didn't find success in theaters, doesn't mean that it isn't worth seeing. Cult classics are classics for a reason, and The Nice Guys definitely deserves its place among the best.

On paper, The Nice Guys has everything necessary to be a successful hit in the theaters. Two movie-star leads, a director with a track record of hits like Iron Man 3 and Kiss Kiss Bang Bang, and a fun story that offered something new as the industry became more franchise-oriented. Unfortunately, it didn't click with moviegoers at the time, earning just $71 million against a $50 million production budget. When you factor in marketing and distribution, the numbers were hardly encouraging.

While it struggled financially, The Nice Guys garnered positive reviews, and its warm reception there helped the film build a more devoted following over the past decade. Viewers have begun discovering what makes the movie such a fun watch: its inventive action sequences, razor-sharp dialogue, and endlessly quotable lines. Much of this appeal stems from Black's understanding of what makes a great buddy comedy. The relationship between Gosling's character Holland March and Crowe's Jackson Healy forms the crux of the film, and their dysfunction is its highlight.

Crowe and Gosling have built their careers playing dramatically different kinds of characters, and their contrasting acting styles are what makes The Nice Guys work so well. In usual fashion, Crowe's Jackson Healy is a physically imposing tough guy in the same vein as films like Gladiator, while Gosling leans on his comedic skills to portray Holland March and serve as the yin to Healy's yang. Neither man is a traditional hero; Healy is brute force, while March is an irresponsible alcoholic whose young daughter often shows smarter judgment than both adults.

Gosling delivers one of the best comedic performances of his career, hinging almost entirely on his talent for physical comedy. Whether he's struggling to maintain his composure in a confrontation or a tense scene, his impeccable timing turns March into an unforgettable character and makes the film one of Gosling's career best.

Meanwhile, Crowe provides the perfect counterbalance as the film's "straight man." His dry, understated reactions make Gosling's character even funnier, as Crowe tries to keep his unlikely partner on task while also serving as another parental figure to March's daughter. Through the laughs, Crowe gives Jackson Healy emotional complexity that keeps him from falling into traditional tough-guy tropes.

Crucially, their relationship develops organically as the film progresses, while the investigation unfolds. Beneath the insults they throw at one another, their violent encounters (including when Healy breaks March's arm, in one of the movie's funniest scenes), and incompetence, The Nice Guys is ultimately about two men who are at their lowest, discovering they need one another.

Underneath the jokes and explosive action, the genuine detective mystery is precisely why The Nice Guys works and is worth repeated viewings. Ten years after its release, the film's renewed streaming popularity feels like a late-stage victory for a movie most once overlooked. While many fans remain hopeful for a sequel, the movie works perfectly well on its own. At the end of the day, sometimes all a great film needs is a second chance.

Savannah Chrisley’s Estranged Sister Lindsie Reacts to Her Pregnancy

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Savannah Chrisley's estranged sister Lindsie Chrisley called out "heartbreaking" backlash over the Growing Up Chrisley star's pregnancy with husband Jason Galanis.

Lindsie Chrisley is sending her best wishes to Savannah Chrisley.

Despite their estrangement, the 37-year-old shared a supportive message to Savannah, 29, amid her pregnancy with husband Jason Galanis, who was imprisoned with their dad Todd Chrisley before receiving a presidential pardon last year.

"I want to congratulate my sister on her pregnancy," Lindsie began on the Nov. 4 episode of her Southern Tea podcast, before addressing online criticism over her sister's baby news. "The things that I have seen posted all week long is truly heartbreaking to me. I don't know why people relish in other people's downfall, and it's not a downfall, right?"

She continued, "To take such a bright, happy moment and something that somebody has waited for such a long period of time, and to turn that into a traumatic experience is awful."

Lindsie also briefly touched on Savannah's relationship with Jason—who the Growing Up Chrisley star initially said she was engaged to in her pregnancy announcement before revealing that the pair had quietly married earlier this year—congratulating the couple on their "engagement or marriage, whichever that may be."

"Things have kind of fallen into place where they’re supposed to for her,” Lindsie added. “And for that, I’m happy for her.

So, does this mean she and Savannah have ended their family feud? Not quite.

Savannah recently gave an update on their longtime estrangement, emphasizing that she and Todd have "not been afforded the luxury of having a relationship" with Lindsie's 14-year-old son Jackson with ex-husband Will Campbell.

"If we had a relationship with him, it would be that normal grandparent, normal him being my nephew," she told her dad, who shares Lindsie with his ex-wife Teresa Terry, on her Unlocked podcast's Nov. 3 episode. "We would have that there. And when we did have a relationship with him, it was all the gifts and all the things that grandparents would do."

For Todd, who shares Savannah with wife Julie Chrisley, he hopes that he "at some point” will "be able to have a relationship" with his grandson.

"I don't talk about Jackson out of respect for him," the 57-year-old explained. "I don't owe the world an explanation. I have not been afforded that luxury of having a relationship with Jackson that did not consist of huge boundaries and walls."

That's why Savannah is looking forward to her own baby boy building a bond with her parents.

"You are not defined by anyone's past, anyone's mistakes, or anyone's opinions," she wrote in an Oct. 28 Instagram Story addressed to her little one on the way. "No amount of noise in this world will ever overshadow the joy of being your mama. You are my answered prayer. And I will never apologize for celebrating you."

For a deeper dive into the Chrisley family dynamics, read on.

Todd Chrisley is the patriarch of the Chrisley family.He was born in Georgia in April 1969 and raised in South Carolina. He and first wife Teresa Terry welcomed daughter Lindsie Chrisley in 1989 and son Kyle Chrisley in 1991 before splitting a few years later. After their breakup, Todd wed Julie Chrisley (born in South Carolina in January 1973) in 1996.Together, they added to their family with the births of kids Chase Chrisley, Savannah Chrisley and Grayson Chrisley and the adoption of Kyle's daughter Chloe Chrisley. They also went through challenges, including Julie's 2012 breast cancer battle (she's now cancer-free).As Todd said on the premiere of Chrisley Knows Best—a show that followed the family's ups and downs as well as their rich lifestyle in Atlanta and later Nashville—he made "95 percent" of their money through his real estate business before entering reality TV. As for Julie, she was working at a bank when she started dating Todd. Chrisley Knows Best ran from 2014 to 2023 and led to spin-offs like Growing Up Chrisley and What's Cooking With Julie? as well as other media ventures like the family members' podcasts.In 2022, Todd and Julie were convicted of fraud and tax crimes, and they were originally sentenced to 12 and seven years in prison, respectively (with him being held in Florida and her being behind bars in Kentucky). The couple pleaded not guilty.In May 2025, President Donald Trump pardoned Todd and Julie and they were released from prison.

Born in September 1989, Lindsie is Todd's eldest daughter from his marriage to Teresa.She attended Georgia State University, where she met now-ex husband Will Campbell. They wed in 2012, and she graduated and gave birth to their son Jackson that December. Two years later, Chrisley Knows Best premiered.Lindsie appeared on the series for five seasons, where viewers saw how Todd and Will didn't get along. She and Will filed for divorce in 2016, and she left the show in 2017.In the years following, there was public fallout between her and her family. It wasn't until 2022 that Lindsie, Todd, Julie and Savannah reunited publicly on crossover episodes of their podcasts.However, their reunion was short-lived. In a 2025 episode of her podcast The Southern Tea, Lindsie said she hasn't seen her dad "in quite some time." The following month, she confirmed she's also estranged from her siblings."We can live in the same space doing the same things and not necessarily agree or align and it’s no hate, it’s no ill will," Lindsie said on Catelynn Lowell and Tyler Baltierra’s podcast, Cate & Ty Break It Down in 2025. "We just don’t serve each other in each other’s lives."In May 2026, Lindsie was arrested on suspicion of DUI, attempting to elude police, improper passing and reckless driving and speeding."I got pulled over speeding past a car on a two lane road," she told TMZ, "because they almost hit an animal and I was trying to miss hitting them and whatever the animal was."

Kyle, born in August 1991, is Todd’s eldest son from his former marriage to Teresa. In November 2012, he became father to daughter Chloe, whose mother is his ex Angela Johnson. But in 2016, Todd and Julie adopted Chloe.While Kyle appeared on only the first season of Chrisley Knows Best (aside from a brief cameo in 2021), his past arrests and rocky relationship with his family (which involved them being estranged and reuniting at various points in time) continued to make headlines. He’s also spoken candidly about his mental health journey and battle with addiction, sharing in a 2019 Facebook post that he's sober.In 2021, Kyle wed Ashley Nelson and became a father to her kids Haiden and Mackenzie.As for where Kyle stands with Todd and Julie, he expressed his hope to reconnect with them after their return from prison."I know they can't wait to get home and just be with family," he told E! News in May 2025, "and I'm going to remain hopeful that another reconciliation is possible."

Chase is the first child Todd welcomed with wife Julie, arriving in June 1996.In addition to appearing on Chrisley Knows Best with his parents and siblings, the entrepreneur co-starred on Growing Up Chrisley with his sister Savannah from 2019 to 2022. Chase was previously engaged to Emmy Medders. But after breaking up and getting back together, they officially parted ways in 2023.After Chase's parents entered prison, he spoke about how he and his siblings came together."We've definitely gotten a lot closer," he told Extra in 2023. "When you get hit with something like this, I feel like a lot of people either separate and go their own ways or they cling together, which is how we were raised. We've clung together and we're still fighting."Chase had his own run-in with the law in January 2025, when he was arrested in Atlanta on a simple battery charge for allegedly becoming "really drunk" and slapping a bar manager after being asked to leave, according to police records obtained by E! News. His lawyers told People that "this incident has been entirely blown out of proportion.""He did not assault anyone," they added. "Chase was trying to close out his tab and leave. We will be independently investigating the situation."

U.S. tech sell-off spills into crypto: Solana slides to $89.63 as AI spending fears mount

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Read the best crypto stories of the day in less than 5 minutes. Join to get it daily in your inbox.

Following the U.S. midterm elections, U.S. tech stocks experienced a sharp sell-off, especially after investors grew increasingly anxious over the political and economic landscape and questioned whether heavy capital flow into AI infrastructure could generate returns in the near term.

The Nasdaq dropped 5.5%, marking its biggest one-day loss in 18 months and making investors more cautious. This sell-off also affected the crypto market, with Solana [SOL] falling 6.86% to $89.63 in the last 24 hours.

What made the decline worse were concerns within the Solana ecosystem. Notably, lending protocol Loopscale stopped operations after a security issue, while an upcoming validator upgrade deadline created more uncertainty for the network.

At the same time, over $11 million in long SOL positions were liquidated in an hour, thus increasing the selling pressure.

At the time of writing, SOL was trading at $89.63, within the 23.6% Fibonacci level on the daily chart. This recent decline brought the token under serious pressure and close to its swing low of $88.48.

Momentum indicators were also in line with the bearish view. The MACD was clearly showing that the downward momentum was still very much intact and that the sellers were still in complete control of the broader price action.

That made it very difficult for the buyers to get any kind of sustained recovery.

However, the daily OBV showed a slight uptick following its sharp decline during the latest sell-off. While this could suggest that buying activity was beginning to return, the relatively flat movement offered little confirmation of anything meaningful.

For now, it suggested that buyers are fighting hard to defend the $88 level.

AMBCrypto’s look at Solana’s 4-hour chart painted a clearer picture of the altcoin’s immediate price action. Notably, after declining into the 23.6% Fibonacci region, SOL began consolidating instead of extending its losses.

This suggested that even though buyers were attempting to defend the current price region, a convincing recovery remained elusive.

The MACD continued to highlight the token’s bearish momentum, indicating that the broader selling pressure had still not subsided entirely. OBV also trended downward, suggesting that selling activity continued to outweigh buying pressure on the shorter timeframe.

Both the daily and H4 suggested that SOL’s current consolidation would persist in the near term before a bullish reversal can start to form.

Furthermore, the $88.48 swing low is an important level to watch. If SOL manages to defend this region, it could continue consolidating or attempt a relief bounce. However, if lost, it would expose the token to further losses, with $85 being the next potential downside target.

How To Challenge A Monopoly In The AI Age

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

If you were in Silicon Valley in the late 1990s, today’s AI boom might feel familiar. In 1998 and 1999, internet traffic was roughly doubling every six to nine months. That was extraordinary growth in demand, and still, that pales in comparison to what we’re seeing unfold now.

Today, the four largest U.S. cloud companies are looking at more than $700 billion in capital spending this year, and the cumulative AI infrastructure spending is projected to reach $3 trillion to $4 trillion by 2030.

Nvidia arguably fired the starting gun in AI chips when Jensen Huang hand-delivered its DGX-1 to OpenAI in 2016. Today, it is a household name deeply embedded in the AI ecosystem, working with near-exclusive supply and investment deals.

With such a huge market under a seeming monopoly, where can competition — and the innovation that follows it — come from?

Part of the answer is that scale costs you speed, though when going up against an execution machine like Nvidia this is simply the baseline you have to solve for. The pace of change in AI outruns anything we saw in previous tech cycles: Model generations turn over in weeks, not years. The hardware beneath it has to keep shifting to serve them, and designing from first principles has real merit.

Hardware suppliers of the dot-com era followed the same peaks and troughs. Cisco, founded in 1984, dominated internet routing for most of the decade, and in March 2000 it was the highest market capitalization company in the world.

Then Juniper Networks, a startup where I was a founding engineer, shipped its first core router in 1998. Juniper went from a fairly anonymous upstart to 17% of the core router market at the end of 1999, then again to 38% in early 2001.

Challengers now can lean on history for some guidance.

First, don’t build a cheaper version of the incumbent’s product. Build a far better one. At Juniper, our bet was that the then-adolescent version of the internet was going to demand an exponentially higher throughput than before. Introducing an entirely new architectural approach to how routers are built, along with an innovative design philosophy focused on performance and reliability, we didn’t set out to sell a better router, so much as redefine what a core router had to be.

The boom in AI inference is today’s version of that strain on throughput. The initial hyperfixation on training AI models is fading, and the industry is collectively moving on to using them — known as inference. Inference is where AI’s promised productivity gains actually materialize, putting trained models to work across every task. As the enterprise applications for AI grow and become more complex, so too does demand for inference compute.

Google recently reported that it now processes more than 3.2 quadrillion tokens a month, up from 480 trillion a year earlier, as AI moves from chat to agents that plan and act on our behalf. Serving tokens at scale is a different beast from when it was just for model training, with different constraints on memory, power and cost.

The core principle we use applies to all sectors: Challengers have a better chance at optimizing for the market that will come, not the market that is already well-serviced by the incumbent.

Challengers start without the incumbent’s biggest asset: a reputation. Buying from the market leader is the safe choice, so a startup has to replace that trust with evidence. Every technological edge you can demonstrate and validate makes the next conversation easier, and over time that compounds into the credibility that an incumbent may take for granted.

To aid this, make migration as easy as possible. No one is wedded to a product out of loyalty. Inertia is one of the biggest strengths of an incumbent: the ease of continuing to do what has been done.

Unless there are clear economic and operational benefits, scale and performance advantages, more often than not, integrating new systems will not be worth the work involved in switching suppliers. Take any technical friction out of the equation, and you have a fighting chance with your improved product.

The internet transformed the world in ways that we couldn’t have predicted, and I have little doubt that the relentless pace and momentum of AI is resulting in changes that are quickly making our lives and work unrecognizable from the recent past. This is only going to accelerate. Monopolies in frontier technology look insurmountable right up until the work changes. What AI does is rapidly morphing and evolving.

There is comfort in that for founders, and in something else too: A small team can now achieve so much. Challenging Cisco in the late 90s and early 2000s meant building a nimble and dynamic organization, albeit much smaller — one that is far more capable, reactive and product-focused. Juniper numbered a few hundred people by the time it had significant market traction, compared to the thousands of employees at Cisco.

Our own company is now around 120 people, attempting what once took an army. That is what should keep the incumbents watching.

R K Anand is co-founder and chief product officer of Tensordyne, an AI inference company. He was a founding engineer and executive vice president of the data center business unit at Juniper Networks and began his career designing microprocessors at Sun Microsystems.

October was a busy month for U.S. startup funding, led by well-known venture investors with a healthy appetite for big deals.

General Catalyst emerged as the month’s busiest lead dealmaker, by both round count and size. Others who ranked high across multiple rankings include Sequoia Capital, Andreessen Horowitz, Accel and Lightspeed Venture Partners.

Among lead venture investors, General Catalyst, known for investing across stages and sectors, topped the list, leading at least nine known rounds.

Lightspeed Venture Partners was next on the list, with eight lead rounds, followed by Sequoia Capital and Andreessen Horowitz, with seven deals each.

Next on the list was Andreessen Horowitz, which participated in at least 22 qualifying rounds, followed by General Catalyst, with 17.

The True Meaning of Power

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

What the Godfather of Realism Can Teach Trump

“International politics, like all politics, is a struggle for power.” Hans Morgenthau wrote this sentence from his desk in Chicago at the dawn of the Cold War, as the United States and the Soviet Union embarked on an open-ended competition for global dominance. Politics Among Nations, the volume that Morgenthau ultimately produced, would quickly become the most widely adopted international relations textbook in the United States and provide an intellectual framework—realism—that would guide generations of scholars and policymakers.

Although much has changed in the nearly eight decades since Morgenthau’s most influential work was published, the fundamentals remain the same. Human nature, which for Morgenthau drove the struggle for power, is unmoved. States are still the most significant actors in the international system and compete for influence. Great powers continue to drive the course of history. Much of today’s world would seem to affirm Morgenthau’s conclusions: Russia’s attempts to establish a sphere of influence in Eastern Europe, China’s quest for hegemony in East Asia, and the United States’ revival of the Monroe Doctrine in the Western Hemisphere all suggest the enduring primacy of national interests over moral aspirations and international norms.

For many, Morgenthau’s contributions begin and end with his identification of power as the key determinant of state behavior. But this reading misses one of his core observations: that even if humans tend to be power-maximizing creatures, they are also moral beings who seek to limit their own power. Morgenthau’s writings reveal a dialectic between power and morality—two seemingly incompatible concepts that he spent his life attempting to reconcile. Far from romanticizing power, Morgenthau saw the need to tame it.

An immigrant who escaped Nazi Germany and owed his life to the United States, Morgenthau had specific guidance to offer his fellow Americans. Even as he urged the United States to protect its vital interests by maintaining a favorable balance of power in key regions, he implored the country to wield its power prudently. Morgenthau saw the United States as the only country with both the capacity and the will to fight on behalf of transcendent values that he believed power had to serve. He was committed to preserving American power as a check on the violence and bigotry he experienced. Yet he feared that U.S. overreach would hollow out the foundations of that power. This, for Morgenthau, would be a tragedy for humanity.

Perhaps no government has misread Morgenthau more than the second Trump administration. Its officials revel in the exercise of military force around the world, from Iran to Venezuela and the Caribbean, while dismissing concerns of alleged war crimes and threatening long-standing allies. White House Deputy Chief of Staff Stephen Miller typified the Trumpian outlook when, in January, he invoked the “iron laws of the world,” one “governed by strength,” “governed by force,” and “governed by power.”

Some analysts have also erroneously rooted the Trump administration’s actions in realism. A lengthy essay in The New York Times Magazine, for instance, ran under the headline “The Theory That Gives Trump a Blank Check for Aggression.” This is understandable, as the Trump administration has repeatedly claimed its actions are underpinned by “flexible realism.”

But any reading of realism that does not consider the moral dimensions of Morgenthau’s theory is both mistaken and dangerous. When the United States limits its own power, abiding by international norms, consulting allies and partners, and wielding its strength in pursuit of transcendent values, countries find American influence more palatable, which helps sustain it. But if Washington’s actions become untethered from these restraints, others will no longer defer to American prerogatives, and the world will become a more dangerous place.

Returning to the foundations of realism—to the man who introduced this intellectual tradition to Americans—is critical. If Morgenthau were to witness this moment in U.S. history, he would conclude that the country had overlearned his lessons about the importance of power and erred in equating it with the use of force. He would see a need to elevate moral questions and rebuild normative constraints on the exercise of power. And he would warn against foreign entanglements that undermine American democracy, which remains the wellspring of the country’s power. These are the lessons that will help the United States navigate an era of intensified great-power competition, one in which the margin for error is thin and the cost of miscalculation could be catastrophic.

Morgenthau was born into an assimilated, middle-class Jewish family in southern Germany in the winter of 1904. His conservative and authoritarian father, Ludwig, raised his only child to revere the monarchy and take pride in their country’s achievements. Morgenthau’s childhood might have passed as that of a shy, precocious boy in a self-assured bourgeois community, but Germany’s defeat in World War I and the rise of anti-Semitism set his life on a drastically different course.

In 1922, as a top student in his high school, Morgenthau was chosen to address his classmates and neighbors in a public ceremony honoring the school’s namesake. When he began to speak, he noticed that some had turned their backs and others were holding their noses at this “stinking Jew.” Morgenthau finished his remarks, but as he left the square, his classmates hurled anti-Semitic insults and spat on him. Just months later, Hitler descended on Morgenthau’s hometown of Coburg with hundreds of storm troopers, who assaulted protesters while the police looked on. Coburg soon gained the ignominious distinction of being the first town to elect the Nazis to power.

Morgenthau left Coburg hoping to become a writer or poet but was forced to pivot to law after hyperinflation ruined his family’s finances. During his apprenticeship under Hugo Sinzheimer, a leading left-wing labor lawyer, he saw conservative judges undermine the Weimar Republic and aid those who sought its destruction. Morgenthau later reflected in his intellectual autobiography that this led him to conclude that “what was decisive was not the merits of different legal interpretations but the distribution of political power.”

As life for German Jews deteriorated, he fled to Geneva to teach law. Switzerland was unfortunately not far enough away; after Hitler took power in Germany, students began boycotting his classes because they did not want to learn German law from a Jew. He then found a teaching position in Madrid but was displaced by the Spanish Civil War and wandered through Europe before arriving in New York in 1937 as a penniless refugee. Having witnessed the rise of fascism up close, Morgenthau was shocked to find that so many Americans believed that human nature was fundamentally good, that peace was the normal condition of humanity, and that history was a story of progress.

In New York and then Kansas City, Morgenthau grew alarmed as the United States watched Nazi Germany rearm, annex Austria, and invade Czechoslovakia. He believed that a failure to understand human nature had inspired Western democracies’ blind faith in international law and the League of Nations, leading them down the path of least resistance with appeasement of Hitler, straight into the moral abyss.

Shaken by the fall of France in 1940, Morgenthau began writing his first English-language book, taking aim at Americans’ confidence that the methods of the natural sciences could make sense of the social world and that the sheer force of reason would eventually eliminate conflict. In Scientific Man vs. Power Politics, published in 1946, he charged that rationalism and liberalism had misunderstood the nature of man, the nature of the world, and the nature of reason itself. Humans, in addition to having rational needs, possessed irrational impulses that could not be reasoned away. Man’s “lust for power would be satisfied only … if he became like God.” Countries, like humans, sought to maximize their power over one another, making conflict inevitable.

Scientific Man received disastrous reviews, and Morgenthau later recalled that it “shocked people to no end.” He adjusted his approach, tailoring his message to Americans and packaging it in a textbook that would appeal to their pragmatism. He wanted this book to mark an evolution in American thinking about foreign policy, privately writing that it would differ from its predecessors “as an automobile differs from a horse and buggy.” Two years later, in Politics Among Nations, he achieved that goal. Morgenthau stressed that the struggle for power was inescapable. All states sought to keep, increase, or demonstrate their strength. Disarmament, collective security, and international law would not contain this struggle. Only a balance of power could prevent countries from attempting to overthrow the existing order. Power could be tamed only by countervailing power.

It is easy to see why many misread Morgenthau as a champion of realpolitik—an heir to the likes of Machiavelli or Bismarck. After all, he placed power at the center of his analysis. But he never romanticized it. As he wrote in a private letter, “I am an analyst of the nation state and of power and have emphasized time and again their negative moral connotations.” Morgenthau’s personal encounters with power had taught him that its unbridled pursuit led to calamity. He understood that normative and moral limitations were required to keep the struggle for power within tolerable bounds. As he wrote in another letter, “It is the task of civilized societies to temper the power drives and direct them into socially useful channels.”

Indeed, Morgenthau had less tolerance for those who failed to reconcile power with morality than for those who underestimated power’s importance. Although President Woodrow Wilson’s universalism and idealism made him one of Morgenthau’s favorite targets, he preferred him to a Machiavellian prince: “Wilson’s failure was at least the failure of greatness, of too great a vision. Nobody will say as much for the epigones of Machiavelli.” He similarly criticized the British historian and political theorist E. H. Carr, whose moral relativism had led him to advocate for appeasing Hitler. Carr, he wrote, had “no transcendent point of view from which to survey the political scene and to appraise the phenomenon of power.” While both men are seen as pivotal realist thinkers, Morgenthau witheringly concluded of Carr that “it is a dangerous thing to be a Machiavelli. It is a disastrous thing to be a Machiavelli without virtù”—that is, it is ruinous to practice power politics without skill and judgment.

In Politics Among Nations, Morgenthau took issue with leaders who both glorify the struggle for power and reject any limits on their own use of power. He argued that “philosophies and political systems which have made the lust and the struggle for power their mainstay have proved impotent and self-destructive.” And he drew attention to common moral standards that could sustain order and moderate the struggle for power. He observed that throughout history, humans insisted on subjecting political action to ethical scrutiny. When such restraints held, countries did less than their power enabled them to do; once commonplace practices, such as assassinating a rival country’s leaders or killing a large portion of its civilian population to reduce its strength, became stigmatized. Morgenthau concluded, “What has changed is the influence of civilization which makes what is desirable and feasible ethically reprehensible and, hence, normally impossible of execution.”

In the immediate aftermath of World War II, however, he believed that the limitations on the struggle for power had reached their weakest point since the advent of the modern state system. Hitler had attempted to extinguish a whole people, and both the Axis and the Allied powers had bombed cities indiscriminately. Although Morgenthau believed the Allies were morally justified in taking such actions to defeat a genocidal regime, he nonetheless feared that the struggle for power was on its way to becoming boundless. In the nuclear age, failing to reestablish these moral barriers could mean the end of humanity.

Morgenthau’s hard-nosed reputation has long obscured the moral core of his realism. He was unequivocal that power must be a means to an end—“an attempt to realize moral values through the medium of politics.” He recognized that maximizing power for its own sake was both destructive and pointless, arguing that “a nation must pursue its interests for the sake of a transcendent purpose which gives meaning to the day-to-day operations of its foreign policy.” In his view, ancient Greece and Rome are remembered because their power served a purpose that outlived their empires: Athenian democracy and philosophy, and Roman law and republican government.

As he assimilated and raised a family in Chicago, Morgenthau came to see the United States as the inheritor of this tradition. The country’s contribution to the world and the source of its power lay in its democracy and civic life. A realist approach—one that prudently wielded power abroad—was best suited to protect the American experiment at home, enabling Americans to live in freedom and security while offering an example for other countries to emulate. In other words, American power had to be bound to purpose—a purpose the country fulfilled “as a light to attract strangers, not as a flame to be spread throughout the world.” This was not merely the observation of a dispassionate scholar. The United States had accepted Morgenthau when no other country would, and it was a place where immigrants like him could rise to the top of their fields.

By the 1960s, Morgenthau saw that American power faced a threat of its own making. He began to develop deep misgivings about U.S. intervention in Vietnam, warning as early as 1961 that it could become a quagmire reminiscent of France’s war in Algeria. Two years later, as clashes between the South Vietnamese government and protesters intensified, Morgenthau urged the United States to get out. He built his opposition on realist grounds, arguing that a communist takeover of Vietnam would not affect the balance of power in Asia, thus rejecting the domino theory, which held that communism would spread inexorably outward once it gained a regional foothold. Morgenthau also recognized that communism was no longer monolithic and believed that Ho Chi Minh could lead a nonaligned country as Tito did in Yugoslavia. He saw the conflict as a civil war and worried that the United States was attempting to maintain a status quo that was itself the source of instability.

Successive administrations failed to heed Morgenthau’s warnings and instead plunged the United States deeper into the war. He came to resent American policymakers’ misappropriation of his lessons on power and entered public debate with new vigor. At a packed rally in New York’s Madison Square Garden in June 1965, he warned that a guerrilla war could be won only through the indiscriminate killing of the South Vietnamese people, which would tear at the moral fiber of the United States. Later that month, Morgenthau debated President Lyndon Johnson’s national security adviser, McGeorge Bundy, on national television. He led with his moral outrage: “I am opposed to our present policy in Vietnam on moral, military, political, and general intellectual grounds.”

The greatest risk of the war, Morgenthau discerned, was “the risk to ourselves, to our mission in the world, to our very existence as a distinct nation.” He lamented, “Here is the last best hope of the downtrodden and the enslaved, to which men of good will throughout the world have looked as a shining example, relieving its frustration in blind ideological fury and aimless destructiveness upon a helpless people.” Vietnam, in his telling, had corrupted the very purpose that gave American power its meaning.

The Vietnam War validated Morgenthau’s belief that power untethered from questions of morality would lead to overreach. It proved that the United States had an interest in constraining itself, if only to avoid disaster. More fundamentally, Vietnam showed that misadventures abroad could boomerang, weakening American democracy and societal cohesion and, ultimately, undermining the country’s international standing.

The second Trump administration’s foreign policy record has thrown these insights into sharp relief. Its officials view questions of morality as a costly sideshow, the remit of woolly-minded idealists who do not understand how the world works. The White House has time and again resorted to military force, striking boats in the Caribbean, kidnapping Venezuelan President Nicolás Maduro, blockading Cuba, and waging war on Iran. It has also threatened military action against Canada and Denmark, two of the country’s long-standing allies.

American democracy, far from serving as “a light to attract strangers,” is fast becoming a cautionary tale. The second Trump administration has undermined the foundations of American power through attacks on the courts, the press, and universities. It has gleefully bulldozed through any checks on its writ. Its assault on institutions and its aggressive foreign policy are intertwined; the administration not only failed to seek congressional authorization for the war in Iran, but it also refused to even make the case to the American people before striking.

President Donald Trump has justified his aggression on the basis of the “iron laws that have always determined global power.” Morgenthau would not have recognized this as realism. He contended that “superior power gives no right, either moral or legal, to do with that power all that it is physically capable of doing.” The Trump administration, by contrast, seems to believe that to not use the power at its disposal is to waste it. Morgenthau’s verdict on Carr applies equally to Trump: he is a “Machiavelli without virtù,” playing power politics without judgment and leading his country to disaster.

Morgenthau would argue that the Trump administration misunderstands the very concept of power, which should not be equated with military might and must instead be understood as a complex psychological relationship. Real power, Morgenthau insisted, lies in the ability to influence a country through consent, not coercion. In this view, the use of force signifies the abdication of political power rather than its culmination. The Trump administration’s belligerence betrays its weakness: its acts of aggression around the world are confessions of its inability to shape events through other means.

Although Morgenthau grew up on the statecraft of Bismarck, he came to admire Abraham Lincoln above all others, even attempting to write a book about him toward the end of his life. Morgenthau saw in Lincoln a rare leader who was never possessed by power, instead possessing it and, ultimately, subordinating it to transcendent values. Today, just as at the height of the Vietnam War, Morgenthau would see his task as restoring morality and ethics to their rightful place and highlighting the need for power to move the world toward justice.

The United States has lost its purpose, and unless it rediscovers it, discrete foreign policy successes will be irrelevant. As Morgenthau wrote, if the country loses its soul, any victories are merely “footnotes to the history of the decline and fall of a once great nation.”

DAVID SACKS is Senior Fellow for Asia Studies at the Council on Foreign Relations and the author of the forthcoming book The Realist: Hans Morgenthau and the Purpose of American Power.

Don’t Soak the Rich

📅 2026-11-06  |  Read Source →

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

Daniel WALDENSTROM is Professor of Economics at the Research Institute of Industrial Economics in Stockholm. He is the author of Richer and More Equal: A New History of Wealth in the West.

Weak Institutions—Not Private Fortunes—Deform Democracy

As private wealth continues to grow to levels never before seen in modern history, a powerful idea is taking hold in the West: the accumulation of great fortunes poses an existential threat to democracy. This idea is not novel. Over a decade ago, the political scientists Jacob Hacker and Paul Pierson asserted that the rise of plutocracy has been a defining trend of American politics since the late 1970s. The economist Paul Krugman argued in 2011 that the “extreme concentration of income is incompatible with real democracy.”

Lately, however, it has begun to gain traction in the halls of power of Western democracies. In his farewell address, former U.S. President Joe Biden argued that an “oligarchy of extreme wealth, power, and influence” was threatening “our entire democracy, our basic rights and freedoms.” Spanish Prime Minister Pedro Sánchez echoed the sentiment at a 2025 G-20 summit, warning that concentrated wealth “corrodes democratic institutions.”

Some now see confiscation as the answer. After a bitter debate and over $300 million of cumulative spending by supporters and opponents, Californians rejected a statewide “billionaires’ tax” ballot measure in this week’s midterm elections. But the proposal counted among its proponents six Nobel laureates in economics, including Daron Acemoglu, Peter Diamond, and Joseph Stiglitz, who endorsed the tax in a letter framing it as “a turning point in the battle between democracy and oligarchy.” The French economist Gabriel Zucman called the measure’s defeat “just the beginning” and vowed that though “Silicon Valley billionaires have won this battle … they will lose this war.” Several European political leaders, including the former German Green Party leader Annalena Baerbock and Danish Prime Minister Mette Frederiksen, have floated wealth tax proposals of their own in recent years.

The risk they have identified is genuine. Democratic societies are not doing enough to prevent the superrich from unduly influencing politics. But their diagnosis—not to mention their preferred solution—is incorrect. Billionaires’ participation in politics does not in itself indicate that a democracy has been captured by moneyed interests. The strength of the political and civil institutions meant to protect against the exercise of those interests is a far more important indicator.

Instead of imposing wealth taxes, countries that are serious about safeguarding their political systems should take their cues from those democracies that have successfully insulated their politics from the influence of large fortunes. They should adopt transparent election laws and restraints on private campaign contributions and encourage a strong, independent media. Such measures will help make societies more democratic. Taxing wealth will simply make them poorer.

Arguments in favor of taxing wealth in the name of democracy often hinge on an understanding of billionaires as a monolithic bloc, committed to an agenda that runs counter to the public interest. These claims contain a kernel of truth. According to the benchmark V-Dem and Freedom House indices, the United States has indeed been in a state of democratic decline since the early 2010s. But it does not follow that billionaires as a group are responsible for this decline—or that such a unified group even exists.

To empirically determine its existence, I classified the richest Americans in the 2026 Forbes 400 listing according to their traceable political donations, analyzing both their average annual giving and their total giving over the past three decades. This method is no doubt imperfect—donations can be informal or hidden and are not the only way to achieve political influence—but the pattern that emerges should nonetheless cast doubt on the idea of a single billionaire class marching on Washington.

The evidence shows that roughly 30 individuals, or eight percent of U.S. billionaires in the Forbes 400, are high-level donors, giving more than $1 million each year for political purposes. Roughly half of American billionaires give on average less than $30,000 a year, 35 percent give less than $10,000, and 15 percent, or 60 of the listed billionaires, have no visible political footprint. Just 16 donors—four percent—have given more than $50 million over the last 30 years. This small minority may well plow enough money into elections to make up for the majority of billionaires with no apparent interest in politics (although if Elon Musk’s midterm outlay is any indication, even vast sums of money are no guarantee of political outcomes). But the very fact that both these groups exist suggests that the presence of the ultrawealthy alone is not necessarily responsible for American democratic decay. The weakness of the institutions meant to check their influence, on the other hand, is.

For the American billionaires who do engage in politics, federal rules and institutions make it easy to achieve disproportionate influence through large-scale private spending. Super PACs are permitted to receive unlimited contributions from individuals, corporations, unions, and other political committees. The hollowing out of independent public-service media and the consolidation of private media make matters worse, allowing a small number of wealthy owners to shape the dominant narrative unchecked.

Much of the momentum behind a wealth tax comes from the United States. But it is hardly the only country with a high proportion of superwealthy residents. Sweden and Switzerland have among the most billionaires per capita in the world, with around 4.7 and 5.0 billionaires per million inhabitants, respectively—higher than the United States’ 2.9 and dwarfing the average of 1.3 for Organization for Economic Cooperation and Development (OECD) countries. If wealth tax proponents were correct about the relationship between extreme wealth and democratic health, one would expect both Sweden’s and Switzerland’s democracies to be in dire shape.

But in neither country has the presence of large private fortunes significantly damaged democracy. Both rank highly among liberal democracies when assessing the fairness of elections, the rule of law, checks on the executive, trust in government, and media freedom. Sweden ranks fifth in the 2026 World Press Freedom Index, and Switzerland ranks eighth. As of 2025, they were tied for sixth in Transparency International’s Corruption Perceptions Index; the United States ranked 29th. According to the OECD’s 2025 trust survey, 62 percent of Swiss respondents had confidence in their national government, the highest of all countries polled. Sweden, at 42 percent, ranked 15th among the countries polled, higher than the OECD average of 40 percent and far exceeding the United States’ 27 percent, as measured by the Partnership for Public Service.

It might be tempting to point to tax policy as an explanation. Indeed, social democratic Sweden, with its 41 percent tax-to-GDP ratio as of 2024, has one of the world’s highest tax levels. Switzerland, however, maintains a tax-to-GDP ratio of roughly 27 percent, well below the OECD average of 34 percent and about even with the U.S. level of 25 percent. That these countries can support large entrepreneurial fortunes without sacrificing their democracies is in fact not the result of draconian restrictions on how those fortunes are spent in politics. Two other shared institutional features offer more compelling answers.

In Sweden, political parties receive funding mainly through public structures. Private contributions are allowed, but individual donors represent a relatively small share of total party funding, and disclosure rules make all large private contributions visible. Sweden’s constitution grants citizens and journalists the right to access almost any official document. Ministers hold little direct influence over administrative agencies, allowing the agencies to operate independently of executive authority. These rules do not eliminate lobbying, but they largely prevent private money from discreetly entering and influencing the political process.

Switzerland’s barriers between private wealth and the levers of government, although similarly sturdy, stop money from influencing politics in slightly different ways. The country’s federal system diffuses power across 26 regional cantons and over 2,000 municipalities, and frequent regional and federal referendums give citizens veto power over legislation. Switzerland has no direct federal funding of parties and no general spending ceiling, but national disclosure rules introduced in 2022 require major campaigns and large donors to identify their financing. The country does have a wealth tax dating back to the nineteenth century, but it is administered at the canton level and its highest marginal rate is nowhere near as punitive as the California proposal. Dispersing authority and enshrining accountability over government decisions through direct democracy are far more responsible for the success of Swiss democracy.

Both countries also have long traditions of private journalism and independent public-service media. A mix of private outlets and public-service media, which has been shown to foster greater public trust in the news and resilience to online disinformation, prevents any single news provider from monopolizing the airwaves or suppressing information.

These institutions are not perfect, and private donations still play a role in Swedish and Swiss politics. That countries with nearly twice the number of billionaires per capita as the United States have thriving democracies should, however, give the lie to the idea that the superrich, by their mere existence, deform democracy.

The proposals currently in vogue are presented as a novel solution to the novel problem of concentrated wealth. But societies have attempted to tax wealth before. Throughout the twentieth century, many European countries imposed similarly designed taxes on household net wealth for redistributive purposes. Problems quickly emerged. Taxing individuals who rarely generated cash income and whose fortunes were thus not easily estimated outraged entrepreneurs, lowered investment, and hampered growth. In the 1970s and 1980s, governments on both the left and the right took note. They tried to discount certain business assets and exempt closely held firms from taxation to mitigate the distortions. But tinkering around these edges only caused new problems of unfairness. Revenues, which at that time constituted less than half a percent of GDP across Europe, compared with total taxes that often stood at 40 percent of GDP, slumped. Ultimately, most countries scrapped the tax.

Many of today’s proponents are more likely to cite the threat of unprecedented fortunes to democracy than the need for redistribution as the impetus for a wealth tax. But they offer no new solutions to the problems of old. Nor would a wealth tax stop billionaires from financing politics, since the wealthiest hardly need to throw the full weight of their fortunes around to wield political influence.

Supporters might argue that the pro-democracy case for a wealth tax is much broader in scope. After all, the superrich exert influence on politics in more ways than just their donations. A wealth tax might not stop a billionaire from buying a state election, but it might stop one from, for example, buying Paramount or The Washington Post. Imperfect a solution as it is, a billionaires’ tax referendum represents a more achievable goal than amending the Constitution to wrest power from the executive.

But a wealth tax has never been an efficient fiscal tool or a substitute for substantive democratic renewal. Whatever small democratic gains it might feasibly bring would be outweighed by the threat to business ventures, capital formation, and long-term economic development it would pose. As anything more than a symbolic measure, a tax on uncertain, often illiquid values is essentially a tax on entrepreneurial risk-taking. But the case against it should not be mistaken for a broadside against all capital taxation. Governments should still seek to raise substantial revenue from taxes on the returns on capital and should set rates and brackets how they please. They must not, however, forget the crucial distinction between taxing already realized income and taxing assets that may yet generate new returns.

A better agenda to safeguard democracy against the influence of money should prioritize reforming the institutions whose weaknesses make such influence possible. Limits on campaign and party finance and more transparent disclosure of lobbying and conflicts of interest would, at the very least, make elections more difficult to buy. Increased funding for public media and stricter antitrust rules to block media mergers would create a more diverse landscape of both private and independent public-service outlets. These measures would affect just the few American billionaires who plow significant parts of their fortunes into politics, leaving the majority of the country’s wealthiest to keep doing what they do best—creating value and driving growth.

Democracies should, of course, always fear oligarchy. They should actively work to curtail the influence of money on politics. They should not, however, confuse economic success with political capture. Countries need entrepreneurs who are willing to take risks and build wealth. Democracy will survive in an era of extraordinary fortunes not by antagonizing successful people but by making political power harder to buy.
